\mathbb{K}^{G}
-stability of
{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}}
, the first two arrows are equivalences.
The whole construction is natural in
B
and provides the equivalence claimed in Assertion
2
.

In order to show Assertion
1
we show that

{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}}\circ\widetilde{\mathrm{Ind}}_{H{,s}}^{G}\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BH,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})\to{\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}}

(4.9)

is reduced,

\mathbb{K}^{{H}}
-stable and semiexact. Then we apply the universal property of
\mathrm{kk}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}
stated in Theorem
1.5
.

Since
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ind}}_{H{,s}}^{G}(0)=0
and
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}
is reduced, the functor in (
4.9
) is reduced as well.

Since the operations
-\otimes_{\min}K(V_{G})
and

\mathrm{Ind}_{H{,s}}^{G}
(as seen in the proof of Lemma
4.11
) preserve semisplit exact sequences
we conclude that
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ind}}_{H{,s}}^{G}
also preserves semisplit exact sequences.
Since
{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}}
is semiexact we conclude that (
4.9
) is semiexact.

The most complicated part of the argument is
\mathbb{K}^{{H}}
-stability.
Let
V\to V^{\prime}
be a unitary inclusion of non-trivial
H
-Hilbert spaces and
B
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(B{H},C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})
.
We must show that the induced map

\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Ind}}^{G}_{H,s}(B\otimes_{\min}K(V)))\to\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Ind}}^{G}_{H,s}(B\otimes_{\min}K(V^{\prime})))

(4.10)

is an equivalence.

If
W
is any
H
-Hilbert space, then we have an isomorphism of
H
-Hilbert spaces

W\otimes L^{2}(H)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{Res}^{H}W\otimes{L^{2}}(H)\,,\quad w\otimes[h]\mapsto{h^{-1}}w\otimes[h]\,,

(4.11)

where
[h]
in
L^{2}(H)
denotes the basis element corresponding to
h
in
H
.
Here
H
acts diagonally on the left hand side, and only on the factor
L^{2}(H)
on the right-hand side

Decomposing
G
into
H
-orbits we obtain an
{H}
-equivariant isomorphism

\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(L^{2}(G))\cong L^{2}(H)\otimes L^{2}(G/H)\,,

(4.12)

where
H
acts by left-translations on the first tensor factor and trivially on the second. Combining (
4.11
) and (
4.12
)
we get an isomorphism of
H
-Hilbert spaces

\displaystyle W\otimes\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(L^{2}(G))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{arwfiojofdafed}}}{{\cong}}W\otimes L^{2}(H)\otimes L^{2}(G/H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{arwfiojofdafedrferferf}}}{{\cong}}\mathrm{Res}^{H}W\otimes L^{2}(H)\otimes L^{2}(G/H)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{arwfiojofdafed}}}{{\cong}}\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(\mathrm{Res}^{H}W\otimes L^{2}(G))\,.

This isomorphism is natural in the
H
-Hilbert space
W
.

We conclude that the homomorphism of
H
-algebras

K(V)\otimes_{\min}\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(K(L^{2}(G)))\to K(V^{\prime})\otimes_{\min}\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(K(L^{2}(G)))

is isomorphic to the homomorphism

\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}K(\mathrm{Res}^{H}V\otimes L^{2}(G)))\to\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}K(\mathrm{Res}^{H}V^{\prime}\otimes L^{2}(G)))\,.

In view of (
4.8
) this implies that
the map

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ind}}_{H,s}^{G}(B\otimes_{\min}K(V))\to\widetilde{\mathrm{Ind}}_{H,s}^{G}(B\otimes_{\min}K(V^{\prime}))

is isomorphic to the map

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{H,s}^{G}(B\otimes_{\min}\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}K(\mathrm{Res}^{H}V\otimes L^{2}(G)))\to\mathrm{Ind}_{H,s}^{G}(B\otimes_{\min}\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}K(\mathrm{Res}^{H}V^{\prime}\otimes L^{2}(G)))\,.

Applying (
4.6
) we furthermore see that the latter is isomorphic to

\mathrm{Ind}_{H,s}^{G}(B)\otimes_{\min}K(\mathrm{Res}^{H}V\otimes L^{2}(G)))\to\mathrm{Ind}_{H,s}^{G}(B)\otimes_{\min}K(\mathrm{Res}^{H}V^{\prime}\otimes L^{2}(G)))\,.

Since the functor
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}
is
\mathbb{K}^{G}
-stable, it sends this map to an equivalence.
Consequently, (
4.10
)
is an equivalence.

∎

The following corollary generalizes the projection formula
[
MN06
, (16)]
from the triangulated to the
\infty
-categorical level.

Corollary 4.13
.

For
?
in
\{\min,\max\}

we have an equivalence of functors

\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}(-)\otimes_{?}(-)\simeq\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}((-)\otimes_{?}\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-))\colon\mathrm{KK}^{H}\times\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}\,.

Proof.

This is
an immediate consequence of Lemma
4.10
,
Lemma
4.6
, Lemma
4.11
and Proposition
1.21
.
∎

For
A
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
we can form the maximal and reduced crossed products
A\rtimes_{\max}G
and
A\rtimes_{r}G
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. In the arguments below we need some details of their construction which we will therefore recall at this point.

Construction 4.14
.

Both crossed products are defined as completions of the algebraic crossed product
A\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G
. The latter is the
*
-algebra generated by elements
(a,g)
with
a
in
A
and
g
in
G
with multiplication
(a^{\prime},g^{\prime})(a,g)\coloneqq(g^{-1}(a^{\prime})a,g^{\prime}g)
and the involution

(a,g)^{*}\coloneqq(g(a^{*}),g^{-1})
subject to the relations
(a,g)+\lambda(a^{\prime},g)=(a+\lambda a^{\prime},g)
for all
a,a^{\prime}
in
A
,
\lambda
in
{\mathbb{C}}
and
g
in
G
. The maximal crossed product
A\rtimes_{\max}G
is the completion of
A\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G
in the maximal norm, and the reduced crossed product
A\rtimes_{r}G
is the completion of

A\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G
in the norm induced by the canonical representation on the
A
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module
L^{2}(G,A)
, see e.g.
[
BE
, Constr. 12.20]
for an explicit formula.

If
H
is a subgroup of
G
, then we have a canonical homomorphism

\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(A)\rtimes_{?}H\to A\rtimes_{?}G

(4.13)

given on generators by

(a,h)\mapsto(a,h)
, where
h
in the target is considered as an element of
G
.

∎

Below we will need the following properties of the crossed products.

Lemma 4.15
.

1.

-\rtimes_{\max}G
preserves filtered colimits and sums.

2.

-\rtimes_{r}G
preserves isometric inclusions and sums.

Proof.

In order to show Assertion
1

we use the fact that the inclusion
\mathrm{incl}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves and detects filtered colimits.
Using
[
Bun
, Prop. 7.3.2]
we see that the composition of
-\rtimes_{\max}G
with this embedding
is equivalent to the composition

{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\circ\mathrm{incl}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BG}}}{{\to}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,

(4.14)

where
L
is the functor from
[
Bun
, (7.1)]
.
It follows from the explicit description of this functor that
L\circ\mathrm{incl}
preserves
filtered colimits. Since
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BG}
preserves all colimits
the composition in (
4.14
) preserves filtered
colimits. We conclude that
-{\rtimes_{\max}}G
preserves filtered colimits.
Since this functor preserves finite sums, it also preserves
arbitrary sums.

We now show Assertion
2
. It is well-known
and easy to check from the explicit description of

-\rtimes_{r}G
that this functor preserves isometric inclusions (see
[
BE
, Prop. 12.24]
for the more general case of
C^{*}
-categories).
In order to see that this functor preserves sums we argue as follows.
If
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
is a family in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
, then considering this family as a
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{C}}((A_{i})_{i\in I})
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
with the set of objects
I
and no non-trivial morphisms
between different objects we have a canonical isomorphism

A({\mathbf{C}}((A_{i})_{i\in I}))\simeq\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i}

(4.15)

in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
, where the functor
A
is
as in Construction
6.1
. By
[
BE
, Lem. 12.22]

the reduced crossed product functor for
C^{*}
-categories
-\rtimes_{r}G:{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
from
[
BE
, Thm. 12.1]

extends the reduced crossed product functor for
C^{*}
-algebras.
Since this extension also sends the isometric inclusions
A_{i}\to{\mathbf{C}}((A_{i})_{i\in I})
to isometric inclusions by
[
BE
, Prop. 12.24]

we can conclude that

{\mathbf{C}}((A_{i})_{i\in I})\rtimes_{r}G\cong{\mathbf{C}}((A_{i}\times_{r}G))\,.

(4.16)

Using that the functor
A
preserves reduced crossed products by
[
BE
, Thm. 12.23]

we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathclap{(\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i})\rtimes_{r}G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{qwefqwdeqdqwedq}}}{{\cong}}A({\mathbf{C}}((A_{i})_{i\in I}))\rtimes_{r}G\cong A({\mathbf{C}}((A_{i})_{i\in I})\rtimes_{r}G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{qefqwefqweqdddwqw}}}{{\cong}}A({\mathbf{C}}((A_{i}\rtimes_{r}G)_{i\in I}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{qwefqwdeqdqwedq}}}{{\cong}}\bigoplus_{i\in I}(A_{i}\rtimes_{r}G)\,.}

∎

Note that it follows from Proposition
7.25

that
-\rtimes_{r}G
preserves filtered colimits of diagrams whose structure maps are isometric inclusions.

The next lemma provides the remaining Assertion
3
of Theorem
1.22
.

Lemma 4.16
.

We have the following commuting diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-\rtimes_{?}G)_{s}}
\scriptstyle{y^{G}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{(-\rtimes_{?}G)_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{incl}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{incl}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{-\rtimes_{?}G}
\textstyle{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\rtimes_{?}G}

(4.17)

where

1.

(-\rtimes_{?}G)_{s}{\colon\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}}
is exact and preserves countable coproducts.

2.

-\rtimes_{?}G{\colon\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to\mathrm{KK}}
preserves colimits and compact objects.

Proof.

The assertion will follow from Lemma
4.4
.
Using that
G
is countable it is easy to check that the functors
-\rtimes_{\max}G
and

-\rtimes_{r}G
preserve separable algebras.
By Lemma
4.15
they preserve sums.
In order to show that they are
\mathrm{Ind}
-s-finitary it remains to
check the Conditions
4.1
.
1
and
4.1
.
2
.

Let
A
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
and
B^{\prime}
be a separable subalgebra of

A\rtimes_{\max}G
. We let
\tilde{B}
be a countable dense subset of
B^{\prime}
.
Then every element
\tilde{b}
in
\tilde{B}
is given by a sum

\tilde{b}=\sum_{g\in G}(\tilde{b}_{g},g)
converging in
A\rtimes_{\max}G
.
We let
A^{\prime}
be the
G
-invariant subalgebra of
A
generated by the elements
\tilde{b}_{g}
for all
g
in
G
in
\tilde{b}
in
\tilde{B}
. By construction it is separable and we have

B^{\prime}\subseteq\overline{A^{\prime}\rtimes_{\max}G}^{A\rtimes_{\max}G}
.
This shows Condition
4.1
.
1
in the case of the maximal crossed product.
The case of
-\rtimes_{\min}G
is analogous.

For
-\rtimes_{\max}G
the
Condition
4.1
.
2
now follows from
Lemma
4.3
since the functor preserves filtered colimits by Lemma
4.15
.
1
.
For
-\rtimes_{\min}G
we again use Lemma
4.3
and the fact
that the functor preserves isometric inclusions by Lemma

4.15
.
2
.

The restriction
(-{\rtimes_{\max}}G)_{s}
preserves semisplit exact sequences by
[
Mey08
, Prop. 9]
. Note that the latter is also true for the reduced crossed product by the same reference.

In order to verify Assumption
4.4
.
3

we will show that the functor

\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}\circ(-\rtimes_{\max}G)_{s}\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}

(4.18)

is reduced,

\mathbb{K}^{G}
-stable and semiexact.
It then follows from
the universal property stated in Theorem
1.5
that it inverts
{\mathrm{kk}_{0}^{G}}
-equivalences.

The functor (
4.18
) and its variant for
\rtimes_{r}
are clearly reduced.

Using that

(-\rtimes_{\max}G)_{s}
sends semisplit exact sequences in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})
to semisplit exact sequences in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and the semiexactness of
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
we see that that the functor (
4.18
) is semiexact. A similar statement holds for the reduced crossed product.

The rest of this argument is devoted to the verification of
\mathbb{K}^{G}
-stability of the functor in (
4.18
) and its reduced version.
This will follow from Lemma
4.17
below and the
\mathbb{K}
-stability of
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.

If
B
is in
{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}
and
A
is in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}})
, then we have a canonical isomorphism

(A\rtimes_{?}G)\otimes_{!}B\cong(A\otimes_{?}B)\rtimes_{!}G\ ,

(4.19)

where
(?,!)\in\{(\max,\max),({\min},r)\}
.
On elementary tensors it is induced by the map

(a,h)\otimes b\mapsto(a\otimes b,h)
.
For a proof in the case
(?,!)=(\max,\max)
(also stated in
[
Mey08
, (3)]
) we refer to
[
Wil07
, Lem. 2.75]
.
For the case
(?,!)=(\min,r)
see
[
Ech10
, Lem. 4.1]
.
In both cases the argument is similar to
the proof of Lemma
4.17
below.
The isomorphism (
4.19
) could be used to show
\mathbb{K}
-stability, but it is not sufficient to show that
(
4.18
) is
\mathbb{K}^{G}
-stable since there we would need it for algebras of the form
K({V})
in place of
B
which have a non-trivial
G
-action.
This problem is settled by the following lemma which is probably well-known to the experts.

Lemma 4.17
.

If
V
is a separable
G
-Hilbert space, then for
?\in\{\max,r\}
we have a canonical isomorphism

(A\otimes K(V))\rtimes_{{?}}G\cong(A\rtimes_{{?}}G)\otimes{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(K(V))}\,.

Proof.

Since
K(V)
is nuclear we can omit the decoration
\min
or
\max
at the tensor products.
As a first step we construct an isomorphism

\phi\colon(A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}K(V))\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G\xrightarrow{\simeq}(A\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G)\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(K(V))}\,.

We define

\phi((a\otimes k,h))\coloneqq(a,h)\otimes hk\,.

It is straightforward to check that
\phi
is a homomorphism
and compatible with the involution.
The inverse of
\phi
is given by

\psi\colon(A\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G)\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(K(V))}\xrightarrow{\simeq}(A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}K(V))\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G\,,\quad\psi((a,h)\otimes k)\coloneqq(a\otimes h^{-1}k,h)\,.

We must show that these isomorphisms extend to the corresponding completions.
We first discuss the case of the maximal crossed product.

Assume that we have a homomorphism of
*
-algebras

(A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}K(V))\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G\to B
with
B
a
C^{*}
-algebra.
We can consider

A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}K(V)
naturally as a subalgebra of
(A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}K(V))\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G
by
a\otimes h\mapsto(a\otimes h,e)
.
The restriction of the representation above extends to
A\otimes K(V)
and then further to
(A\otimes K(V))\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G
, and hence to
(A\otimes K(V))\rtimes G
.

Similarly, consider a
homomorphism of
*
-algebras

(A\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G)\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(K(V))}\to B
. For every finite-dimensional matrix subalgebra
E
in
{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(K(V))}

we get a representation of

(A\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G)\otimes E

by restriction along

(A\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G)\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}E\to(A\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G)\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(K(V))}
.
These representations extend to

(A\rtimes G)\otimes E
. Since every operator in
{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(K(V))}
can be
approximated by finite-dimensional ones we can further extend
the representation above to a representation of

(A\rtimes G)\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(K(V))}
, and hence again to
(A\rtimes G)\otimes{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(K(V))}
.

The two observations above show that
\phi
and
\psi
are isometries with respect to the maximal norms. This finishes the case of the maximal crossed product.

For the reduced crossed product we interpret
\otimes
as the minimal, i.e. spatial, tensor product. The norm on
(A\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G)\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(K(V))}
is induced from the representation on

L^{2}(G,{A})\otimes{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(V)}
, and the norm
on
(A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}K(V))\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G
is induced from
the representation on
L^{2}(G,{A}\otimes V)
. We have an isometry

U\colon L^{2}(G,{A})\otimes{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(V)}\to L^{2}(G,{A}\otimes V)
which sends

\alpha\otimes v
for
\alpha
in
L^{2}(G,{A})
and
v
in
V
to the function
h\mapsto\alpha(h)\otimes{h}v
. The isometry
U
intertwines the representation of
(A\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G)\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(K(V))}
on
L^{2}(G,{A})\otimes{\mathrm{Res}^{G}(V)}
with its representation via
\psi
on
L^{2}(G,{A}\otimes V)
.
Hence
\psi
is isometric for the reduced crossed product and minimal tensor product.
This finishes the argument in the case of the reduced crossed product. ∎

This finishes the proof of Lemma
4.16
.
∎

Thus we have completed the proof of Theorem
1.22
from the introduction.

The existence of a
right-adjoint of
\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}\colon\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to\mathrm{KK}^{H}
for abstract reasons has been observed in Remark
4.5
.
In the following we identify it
explicitly with a functor induced by a functor on the algebra level provided
H
is a subgroup of
G
of finite index.

Construction 4.18
.

Let
H
be any subgroup of
G
.
We define the coinduction functor

\mathrm{Coind}_{H}^{G}\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BH,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\,,\quad A\mapsto\mathrm{Coind}_{H}^{G}(A)\coloneqq C_{b}(G,A)^{H}\,,

where
C_{b}(G,A)^{H}
is the subspace of the algebra

C_{b}(G,A)
of bounded functions
f\colon G\to A
which are
H
-invariant in the sense that
f(gh)=h^{-1}f(g)
for all
g
in
G
and
h
in
H
. The
*
-algebra structure on
C_{b}(G,A)^{H}
is pointwise induced by the
*
-algebra structure on
A
, and the
G
-action is by left-translation of functions.
Note that
C_{b}(G,A)^{H}
is not separable, in general.
We furthermore define a natural transformation

\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}\circ\mathrm{Coind}_{H}^{G}\to{\mathtt{id}}

(4.20)

whose value at
A
is the
H
-equivariant evaluation map

C_{b}(G,A)^{H}\ni f\mapsto f(e)\in A
.

∎

It is straightforward to check that
(
4.20
) is a counit of an adjunction

\mathrm{Res}_{H}^{G}:{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\rightleftarrows{\mathbf{Fun}}(BH,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}):\mathrm{Coind}_{H}^{G}\,,

(4.21)

see also
[
BDS15
, Lem. 4.2]
.

Proposition 4.19
.

If
H
is a subgroup of finite index in
G
, then the adjunction (
4.21
) descends to an adjunction

\mathrm{Res}_{H}^{G}:\mathrm{KK}^{G}\rightleftarrows\mathrm{KK}^{H}:\mathrm{Coind}_{H}^{G}\,.

(4.22)

Proof.

By Lemma
4.6
we know that
\mathrm{Res}_{H}^{G}
descends to the stable
\infty
-categories.
In order to show the proposition
it therefore suffices to show that

\mathrm{Coind}_{H}^{G}
also descends. But this follows from
Lemma
4.11
since we have an isomorphism

\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}\cong\mathrm{Coind}_{H}^{G}
for subgroups
H
of finite index.
∎

Remark 4.20
.

In this remark we explain why it is not clear whether Proposition
4.19
holds true
in the case when the index of the subgroup
H
of
G
is not finite.
We would like to show that

\mathrm{kk}^{G}\circ\mathrm{Coind}_{H}^{G}\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BH,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}

is
s
-finitary, reduced,
\mathbb{K}^{H}
-stable and split exact.
The properties split exact, reduced and

s
-finitary are straightforward. The
problematic property is
\mathbb{K}^{H}
-stability. For example, in order to verify the special case of
\mathbb{K}
-stability,
we
would like to use that the canonical map

{K(\ell^{2})}\otimes C_{b}(G,A)^{H}\to C_{b}(G,{K(\ell^{2})}\otimes A)^{H}

is an isomorphism. But this is wrong,
see e.g.
[
Wil03
]
for a detailed discussion.
In the verification of
\mathbb{K}^{G}
-stablity we would need the analog of Lemma
4.10
for
\mathrm{Coind}_{H}^{G}
in place of
\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}
which fails for a similar reason.

∎

The natural transformation (
4.5
) descends to a natural transformation of functors

\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\iota)\colon{\mathtt{id}}\to\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}\circ\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{H}\colon\mathrm{KK}^{H}\to\mathrm{KK}^{H}\,.

(4.23)

The following proposition settles Assertions
1
and
2
of Theorem
1.23
.

Proposition 4.21
.

1.

The transformation

\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\iota)
is the unit of an adjunction

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{H}:\mathrm{KK}^{H}\rightleftarrows\mathrm{KK}^{G}:\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}\,.

2.

The transformation (
1.17
) naturally induces an equivalence of functors

{-}\rtimes_{?}H\to\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}(-)\rtimes_{?}G\colon\mathrm{KK}^{H}\to\mathrm{KK}

for
?
in
\{{r},\max\}
.

Proof.

For Assertion
1
we
must show that the natural transformation of functors
(\mathrm{KK}^{H})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}

\displaystyle r^{G}_{H}\colon\mathrm{KK}^{G}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{H}(-),-)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{KK}^{H}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(\mathrm{Ind}^{{G}}_{H}(-)),\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-))

(4.24)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\eqref{frqfwefwefwefqef}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{KK}^{H}(-,\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-))\,.

is an equivalence. The domain and the target of (
4.24
) send filtered colimits in
\mathrm{KK}^{H}
to limits in
\mathbf{Sp}
. Since
\mathrm{KK}^{H}
is generated by
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{H}
under filtered colimits it suffices to show that the restriction of
(
4.24
) to

(\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{H})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}^{G}
is an equivalence. Since now both sides preserve filtered colimits in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
it suffices to consider the restriction to

(\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{H})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}
. Finally,
since both sides are compatible with fibre sequences
it actually suffices to show that the transformation

\pi_{0}r^{G}_{H}\colon\pi_{0}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{H}(-),-)\to\pi_{0}\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{H}(-,\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-))

of group-valued functors on
(\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{H})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}

is an isomorphism.
But this is the same as that in
[
MN06
, (20)]
, which is shown to be an isomorphism in loc. cit. This finishes the proof that (
4.24
) is an equivalence and hence of Assertion
1
.

To see the Assertion
2
first observe that the domain and the target of
the transformation are s-finitary.
Hence it suffices to check the equivalence after restriction to
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BH,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})
.
We now use Green’s Imprimitivity Theorem which states that for
A
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BH,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})
(and also more general
H
-
C^{*}
-algebras)
the map of
C^{*}
-algebras

A\rtimes_{?}H\to\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}(A)\rtimes_{?}G

induced by (
1.17
)
is a Morita equivalence and hence a
\mathrm{kk}_{0}
-equivalence. We finally use that
\mathrm{kk}
inverts
\mathrm{kk}_{0}
-equivalences.
8
8

8

The text before
[
MN06
, (9)]

suggests that the authors wanted to state the version of Green’s Imprimitivity Theorem
on the level of their triangulated categories. This version follows from our
\infty
-categorical version by going to the homotopy category.
Our proof is essentially the same argument as envisaged by Meyer–Nest for the justification of
[
MN06
, (9)]
with the crucial addendum, that the Morita equivalence is induced by a morphism on the level of
C^{*}
-algebras.

∎

Remark 4.22
.

The Proposition
4.21
.
1
identifies the right adjoint of
\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}\colon\mathrm{KK}^{H}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}
whose existence was prediced by Remark
4.5
with
\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}\colon\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to\mathrm{KK}^{H}
.

∎

Recall the internal morphism object functor
\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{?}(-,-)
from (
1.15
).

Corollary 4.23
.

For
?
in
\{\min,\max\}
we have an equivalence of functors

\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}\circ\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{?}(-,-)\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{H}_{?}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-),\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-))\colon{(\mathrm{KK}^{G})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to\mathrm{KK}^{H}\,.

(4.25)

Proof.

This is a formal consequence of the adjunction in Proposition
4.21
.
1
together with Corollary
4.13
.
∎

We assume that
H
is a finite group. If
A
is in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, then we
consider the homomorphism

\epsilon_{A}\colon A\to\mathrm{Res}_{H}(A)\rtimes H\,,\quad a\mapsto\frac{1}{|H|}\sum_{h\in H}(a,h)\,.

Recall that
\mathrm{Res}_{H}(A)
denotes the
C^{*}
-algebra
A
equipped with the trivial
H
-action.
The family

\epsilon=(\epsilon_{A})_{A\in C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}
is a natural transformation

\epsilon\colon{\mathtt{id}}\to\mathrm{Res}_{H}(-)\rtimes H

(4.26)

of endofunctors of
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
It gives rise to a natural transformation

\mathrm{GJ}^{H}\colon\mathrm{KK}^{H}(\mathrm{Res}_{H}(-),-)\xrightarrow{-\rtimes_{H}}\mathrm{KK}(\mathrm{Res}_{H}(-)\rtimes H,-\rtimes H)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{kk}(\epsilon)^{*}}\mathrm{KK}(-,-\rtimes H)

(4.27)

of functors from

\mathrm{KK}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}^{H}
to
\mathbf{Sp}
.
Here
\mathrm{GJ}^{H}
stands for Green–Julg.

The first assertion of the following theorem is a spectrum-level generalization of the classical Green–Julg theorem.
For a finite group
H
it explicitly identifies the right-adjoint
of
\mathrm{Res}_{H}\colon\mathrm{KK}\to\mathrm{KK}^{H}
whose existence was predicted by Remark
4.5
.
Recall that
G
is always assumed to be a countable group.

Theorem 4.24
.

Let
H
be a finite group. Then the transformation (
4.26
) induces the unit of an adjunction

\mathrm{Res}_{H}(-):\mathrm{KK}\rightleftarrows\mathrm{KK}^{H}:-\rtimes H\,.

(4.28)

Proof.

We must show that (
4.27
) is an equivalence.
Since the functors in the domain and the target of (
4.27
) send filtered colimits in
\mathrm{KK}
to limits and
\mathrm{KK}
is generated by
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
under filtered colimits it suffices to show that the restriction of (
4.27
)
to
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}^{H}
is an equivalence. Since this restriction preserves filtered colimits in
\mathrm{KK}^{H}
it suffices to consider the restriction to
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{H}
.
Since both sides are compatible with suspensions it
suffices to show that we get an isomorphism of group-valued functors after applying
\pi_{0}
.
It thus suffices to show that we get an isomorphism

K\!K^{H}(\mathrm{Res}_{H}(A),B)\xrightarrow{\cong}K\!K(A,B\rtimes H)

(4.29)

for every
A
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and

B
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})
. This is the classical
Green–Julg theorem (see
[
Bla98
, Thm. 20.2.7]
for the case
A={\mathbb{C}})
.

In the following we sketch the argument.
For
K\!K^{H}
we work with Kasparov
(\mathrm{Res}_{H}(A),B)
-modules
((M,\rho),\phi,F)
such that
F
is
H
-equivariant, i.e.
\rho(h)F=F\rho(h)
for all
h
in
H
.
Here
\rho(h)\colon M\to M
denotes the action of
h
in
H
on
M
such that

\langle\rho(h)(m),m^{\prime}\rangle=h(\langle m,\rho(h^{-1})(m^{\prime})\rangle)

for all
m,m^{\prime}
in
M
. If
H
acts non-trivially on
B
, then
\rho(h)
is not right-
B
-linear in general. Following the definitions the map (
4.29
) sends
the
(\mathrm{Res}_{H}(A),B)
-module

((M,\rho),\phi,F)
to the
(A,B\rtimes H)
-module

(M\rtimes H,(\phi\rtimes H)\circ\epsilon_{A},F\rtimes H)\,.

(4.30)

Here

M\rtimes H
is the Hilbert
(B\rtimes H)
-module whose underlying
{\mathbb{C}}
-vector space is
given by
\bigoplus_{h\in H}M
. We write the generators, which have only one non-zero entry
m
in the summand indexed by
h
,
in the form
(m,h)
. Then the right-action of
B\rtimes H
is given by

(m,h)(b,h^{\prime})=(\rho(h^{\prime,-1})(m)b,hh^{\prime})
. Furthermore, the
(B\rtimes H)
-valued scalar product
is given by
\langle(m,h),(m^{\prime},h^{\prime})\rangle\coloneqq(\langle\rho(h^{\prime,-1}h)(m),m^{\prime}\rangle_{E},h^{-1}h^{\prime})
. The operator

F\rtimes H
acts as
F(m,h)=(F(m),h)
.
Finally, the representation
\phi\rtimes H
of
A\rtimes H
on
M\rtimes H
is given by
(\phi\rtimes H)(a,h)(m,h^{\prime})=(\phi(a)(m),hh^{\prime})
.

In the following we find a simpler representative of the class of (
4.30
) which is more directly related with
((M,\rho),\phi,F)
.
We consider the projection
\pi_{1}\in B(M\rtimes H)
given by

\pi_{1}(m,h)\coloneqq\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}\in H}(\rho(h^{\prime})(m),h^{\prime}h)\,.

This projection commutes with
F\rtimes H
and

(\phi\rtimes H)(\epsilon_{A}(a))
for every
a
in
A
. Furthermore,

\pi_{1}\circ(\phi\rtimes H)(\epsilon_{A}(a))=(\phi\rtimes H)(\epsilon_{A}(a))\quad\text{and}\quad(1-\pi_{1})\circ(\phi\rtimes H)(\epsilon_{A}(a))=0

for all
a
in
A
.
We decompose

\displaystyle(M\rtimes H,(\phi\rtimes H)\circ\epsilon_{A},F\rtimes H)

\displaystyle\cong(\pi_{1}(M\rtimes H),(\phi\rtimes H)\circ\epsilon_{A}

\displaystyle,\pi_{1}\circ(F\rtimes H))\oplus((1-\pi_{1})M\rtimes H,0,(1-\pi_{1})\circ(F\rtimes H))\,.

The second summand is degenerate. Hence the image of

((M,\rho),\phi,F)
under (
4.29
) is also represented by

(\pi_{1}(M\rtimes H),(\phi\rtimes H)\circ\epsilon_{A},\pi_{1}\circ(F\rtimes H))\,.

The map

\psi\colon M\to M\rtimes H\,,\quad m\mapsto\frac{1}{|H|}\sum_{h\in H}(m,h)

induces a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear isomorphism
of
M
with
\pi_{1}(M\rtimes H)
.
For
a
in
A
and
m
in
M
we have

\psi(\phi(a)m)=(\phi\rtimes H)(\epsilon_{A}(a))\psi(m)\,.

Furthermore,

\psi(F(m))=\pi_{1}((F\rtimes H)(\psi(m)))\,.

We equip
M
with the right-
H
-action by

mh\coloneqq\rho(h^{-1})(m)
, then we get a covariant right-representation of
B
on
M
and hence a right-
(B\rtimes H)
-module structure.
We let
M^{\prime}
be
M
with this right
(B\rtimes H)
-module structure.
The map
\psi
then becomes
(B\rtimes H)
-linear from
M^{\prime}
to
\pi_{1}(M\rtimes H)
. If we finally define the
(B\rtimes H)
-valued scalar product on
M^{\prime}
by

\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle^{\prime}\coloneqq\frac{1}{|H|}\sum_{h\in H}(\langle\rho(h)(m),m^{\prime}\rangle_{E},h^{-1})\,,

then
\psi
becomes an isomorphism of right Hilbert
(B\rtimes H)
-modules from
M^{\prime}
to
\pi_{1}(M\rtimes H)
.
We conclude that
the image of

((M,\rho),\phi,F)
under (
4.29
) is represented by the Kasparov

(A,B\rtimes H)
-module

(M^{\prime},\phi,F)
.

For the inverse consider a class in

K\!K(A,B\rtimes H)
represented by

(M^{\prime},\phi,F)
.
We consider
M^{\prime}
as
a right Hilbert
B
-module
M
by restriction along
B\mapsto B\rtimes H
,
b\mapsto(b,1)
.
We further define the map

t\colon B\rtimes H\to B\,,\quad\sum_{h\in H}(b_{h},h)\mapsto b_{e}

and the
B
-valued scalar product on
M
by

\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle\coloneqq t(\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle^{\prime})\,.

We note that the right
B
-module
M
is essential so that
we get a right
H
-action
(m,h)\mapsto mh
on
M
. We then define

\rho(h)(m)\coloneqq mh
.
Then the inverse sends the class of
(M^{\prime},\phi,F)
to the class of

((M,\rho),\phi,F)
.

It is easy to see that these constructions are inverse to each other up to isomorphism. Since they preserve degenerate modules and
are compatible with direct sums and homotopies they induce inverse to each other isomorphisms between Kasparov groups.
This finishes the verification that (
4.29
) is an isomorphism.
∎

Let
B
be in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. Then we have a canonical homomorphism

\lambda_{B}\colon\mathrm{Res}_{G}(B)\rtimes_{\max}G\to B

of
C^{*}
-algebras which corresponds to the covariant representation

({\mathtt{id}}_{B},\mathrm{triv})
consisting of the identity of
B
and the trivial representation of
G
. The family

\lambda=(\lambda_{B})_{B\in C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}
is a natural transformation

\lambda\colon\mathrm{Res}_{G}(-)\rtimes_{\max}G\to{\mathtt{id}}
of endofunctors of
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
The following theorem is the spectrum-version of the dual Green-Julg theorem.
9
9

9

We thank J. Echterhoff for convincing us that the statement is true in this generality.
It explicitly identifies the right-adjoint
of
-\rtimes_{\max}G\colon\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to\mathrm{KK}
whose existence was predicted by Remark
4.5
.

Theorem 4.25
.

The natural transformation
\lambda
is the counit of an adjunction

-\rtimes_{\max}G:\mathrm{KK}^{G}\rightleftarrows\mathrm{KK}:{\mathrm{Res}_{G}}\,.

Proof.

We must show that
the composition

\mathrm{GJ}_{G}\colon\mathrm{KK}^{G}(-,\mathrm{Res}_{G}(-))\xrightarrow{-\rtimes_{\max}G}\mathrm{KK}(-\rtimes_{\max}G,\mathrm{Res}_{G}(-)\rtimes_{\max}G)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{kk}(\lambda)_{*}}\mathrm{KK}(-\rtimes_{\max}G,-)

(4.31)

is an equivalence of functors from
(\mathrm{KK}^{G})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}
to
\mathbf{Sp}
.
In the first step we use the fact that the functors in the domain and target of (
4.31
) send filtered colimits
in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
to limits. Since
\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}
generates
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
under filtered colimits it suffices to show that the restriction of the transformation to
(\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}
is an equivalence. But
this restriction
preserves filtered colimits in
\mathrm{KK}
. Hence it suffices to consider the restriction
to
(\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.
Since both sides are compatible with suspensions
it suffices to check
that we get an isomorphism of group-valued functors after applying
\pi_{0}
.
It thus suffices to show that
we get an isomorphism of groups

K\!K^{G}(A,\mathrm{Res}_{G}(B))\xrightarrow{\cong}K\!K(A\rtimes_{\max}G,B)

(4.32)

for every

A
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})
and
B
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.
This is the classical dual Green–Julg theorem (see
[
Bla98
, Thm. 20.2.7]
for the case
B={\mathbb{C}})
.

For completeness we sketch the argument.
On level of Kasparov modules the map (
4.32
) sends the
(A,B)
-module

((H,\rho),\phi,F)
to the
(A\rtimes_{\max}G,B)
-module
(H,\tilde{\phi},F)
, where

\tilde{\phi}\colon A\rtimes_{\max}G\to B(H)
is the homomorphism canonically induced by the universal property of the maximal crossed product by the covariant representation
(\phi,\rho)
, where

\rho
is the unitary representation of
G
on
H
.
For the inverse we use that we can actually represent every
class in
K\!K(A\rtimes_{\max}G,B)
by an essential module
[
Bla98
, Prop. 18.3.6]
. The
inverse sends such a module

(H,\tilde{\phi},F)
to
((H,\rho),\phi,F)
, where

\phi
is the restriction of
\tilde{\phi}
via the canonical embedding
A\to A\rtimes_{\max}G
which exists since
G
is discrete, and where
\rho
is the unitary representation on

H
induced by

\tilde{\phi}
using its essentialness.
∎

This completes the proof of Theorem
1.23
.

5
Analytic
\bm{K}
-homology

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{LCH}_{+}^{\mathrm{prop}}
and
Y
be a
G
-invariant closed subset of
X
.
Then we have an exact sequence

0\to C_{0}(X\setminus Y)\to C_{0}(X)\to C_{0}(Y)\to 0

(5.1)

in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.
Recall the Definition
1.11
of the notion of split-closedness of
Y
.

Proposition 5.1
.

The closed invariant subset
Y
of
X
is split-closed in the following cases:

1.

G
acts properly on an invariant neighbourhood of
Y
in
X
and
Y
is second countable.

2.

Y
admits a
G
-invariant tubular neighbourhood.

Proof.

We start with the proof of Assertion
1
.
Let
U
be an invariant open neighbouhood of
Y
in
X
.
It suffices to construct an equivariant cpc split

s\colon C_{0}(Y)\to C_{0}(U)
. Composing with the extension-by-zero map
C_{0}(U)\to C_{0}(X)
we then get an equivariant cpc split for the sequence in (
5.1
).

Since
Y
is second countable, the
C^{*}
-algebra
C_{0}(Y)
is separable (see e.g.
[
Cho12
]
).
Since it is also nuclear, we can apply the Choi–Effros lifting theorem

[
CE76
]
(see also
[
Bla06
, IV.3.2.5]
) in order to get
a cpc split
s\colon C_{0}(Y)\to C_{0}(U)
which is not necessarily equivariant.
Since here we work with commutative algebras such a split is cpc if and only if it is contractive and preserves positive functions.

Since
G
acts properly on
U
we can choose a function

\chi
in
C(U,[0,1])
such that the action map

G\times U\to U
restricts to a proper map on
G\times{\mathtt{supp}}(\chi)
and
\sum_{g\in G}g^{-1,*}\chi\equiv 1
.
We now define

\bar{s}\colon C_{0}(Y)\to C_{0}(U)\,,\quad\bar{s}(f)\coloneqq\sum_{g\in G}g^{-1,*}[\chi s(g^{*}f)]\,.

One verifies that this is an equivariant cpc left inverse of the restriction map
C_{0}(U)\to C_{0}(Y)
.

Under Assumption
2

we have an invariant tubular neighbourhood
U

of
Y
, an invariant retraction map
r\colon U\to Y
, and an invariant radial function

\rho\colon U\to[0,\infty)
such that
r_{|\rho^{-1}([0,1])}
is proper.
We choose a function
\chi\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
such that
\chi(0)=1
and
\chi(t)=0
for
t\geq 1
.
Then we define
s\colon C_{0}(Y)\to C_{0}(U)
by

s(f)\coloneqq\rho^{*}\chi\cdot r^{*}s
.
This is an equivariant cpc split of the restriction

C_{0}(U)\to C_{0}(Y)
.
As above,
composing with the extension-by-zero map
C_{0}(U)\to C_{0}(X)
we get an equivariant cpc split for
(
5.1
).
∎

We consider the functor

\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(-)\coloneqq\mathrm{kk}^{G}\circ C_{0}(-)\colon(G\mathbf{LCH}_{+}^{\mathrm{prop}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}\,.

We furthermore let

G\mathbf{LCH}_{\mathrm{2nd},+}^{\mathrm{prop}}
be the full subcategory of
G\mathbf{LCH}_{+}^{\mathrm{prop}}
consisting of second countable spaces and set

\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}C_{0}(-)\coloneqq\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}\circ C_{0}(-)\colon(G\mathbf{LCH}_{\mathrm{2nd},+}^{\mathrm{prop}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}\,.

These functors can be considered as the universal versions of the analytic
K
-homology functors from (
1.3
) and Definition
1.14
. The following diagram commutes by definition

\textstyle{(G\mathbf{LCH}_{\mathrm{2nd},+}^{\mathrm{prop}})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}C_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{incl}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y^{G}}
\textstyle{(G\mathbf{LCH}_{+}^{\mathrm{prop}})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}

(5.2)

where
y^{G}
is the functor from (
1.6
).

In the following, we list the basic properties of these functors.
The spaces in the statement belong to
G\mathbf{LCH}_{+}^{\mathrm{prop}}
.

Theorem 5.2
.

1.

The functor
\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}
is homotopy invariant.

2.

If
Y
is an invariant split-closed subspace of
X
, then we have a natural fibre sequence

\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(X\setminus Y)\to\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(X)\to\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(Y)\,.

3.

We have
\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}([0,\infty)\times X)\simeq 0
.

4.

If
(X_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
is a family in
G\mathbf{LCH}_{\mathrm{2nd},+}^{\mathrm{prop}}
, then we have a canonical equivalence

\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}C_{0}(X_{n})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}C_{0}\big(\bigsqcup_{n\in{\rm Nature}}X_{n}\big)\,.

5.

The functor
\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}
has a symmetric monoidal refinement

\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}^{\otimes_{?}}\colon{G\mathbf{LCH}_{+}^{\mathrm{prop}}}^{{,\otimes}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G,{\otimes}_{?}}

for
?
in
\{\min,\max\}
.

Proof.

We start with Assertion
1
.
The functor
C_{0}
sends the projection
[0,1]\times X\to X
to the embedding

C_{0}(X)\to C_{0}([0,1]\times X)\cong C([0,1])\otimes C_{0}(X)\,,

which is an instance of (
1.5
).
Since
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
is homotopy invariant, the assertion follows.

We now show Assertion
2
. If
Y
is a split-closed subspace of
X
, then we have a semisplit exact sequence

0\to C_{0}(X\setminus Y)\to C_{0}(X)\to C_{0}(Y)\to 0\,,

(5.3)

where the first map is given by extension by zero, while the second map is the restriction of functions from
X
to
Y
.
We apply the semiexactness of
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
in order to get the desired fibre sequence.

For Assertion
3
we consider

\displaystyle h:C_{0}([0,\infty)\times X)

\displaystyle\to C([0,\infty])\otimes C_{0}([0,\infty)\times X)\cong C_{0}([0,\infty]\times[0,\infty)\times X)

\displaystyle f\mapsto\left((u,t,x)\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}f(t+u,x)&u\not=\infty\\
0&u=\infty\end{array}\right.\right)\ .

By the homotopy invariance of
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
we have an equivalence

\mathrm{kk}^{G}({\mathtt{ev}}_{u=0})\circ\mathrm{kk}^{G}(h)\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{G}({\mathtt{ev}}_{u=\infty})\circ\mathrm{kk}^{G}(h)
.
But
{\mathtt{ev}}_{u=\infty}\circ h=0
and
{\mathtt{ev}}_{0}\circ h={\mathtt{id}}_{C_{0}([0,\infty)\otimes X)}
. This implies that

\mathrm{kk}^{G}({\mathtt{id}}_{C_{0}([0,\infty)\otimes X)})=0
and hence the Assertion.

For Assertion
4
we first observe that

C_{0}\big(\bigsqcup_{n\in{\rm Nature}}X_{n}\big)\cong\bigoplus_{n\in{\rm Nature}}C_{0}(X_{n})\,.

We then use that
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}
preserves countable sums by Theorem
1.4
.
6
.

For Assertion
5
we use that the functor

C_{0}\colon({G\mathbf{LCH}_{+}^{\mathrm{prop}}}^{{,\otimes}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})^{\otimes_{?}}

is symmetric monoidal, where the structure map is induced by

C_{0}(X)\otimes_{?}C_{0}(X^{\prime})\to C_{0}(X\times X^{\prime})\,,\quad f\otimes f^{\prime}\mapsto((x,x^{\prime})\mapsto f(x)f(x^{\prime}))\,,

see Remark
1.16
for the symmetric monoidal structure on
G\mathbf{LCH}_{+}^{\mathrm{prop}}
.
This is true in both cases
?=\min
and
?=\max
. We then use Proposition
1.21
stating that
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
is symmetric monoidal.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

In Assertion
5.2
.
2
we require that
Y
is split-closed. In fact, for an arbitrary invariant closed subset
Y
of
X

we do not know whether the sequence (
5.3
) is semisplit.
Furthermore, note that in Assertion
5.2
.
4
we must restrict to countable unions of
second countable spaces since we only know that
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}
preserves countable sums, while
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
is not expected to have this property.
Finally note that all the assertions
stated for
\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}
have an obvious version for
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}C_{0}
.
∎

Example 5.4
.

For every
X
in
G\mathbf{LCH}_{\mathrm{2nd},+}^{\mathrm{prop}}
and
n
in
{\rm Nature}
we have
an equivalence

\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times X)\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(X)\,.

(5.4)

In order to see this we argue by induction on
n
.
The assertion is evidently true for
n=0
. We now assume that
n>0
and consider the commutative square

\textstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}([0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}((-\infty,0]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times X)}

where the maps are induced by the obvious inclusions of closed subspaces which are split-closed by Proposition
5.1
.
2
. Since

((-\infty,0]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times X)\setminus({\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times X)\cong({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times X)\setminus([0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1})\cong(-\infty,0)\times X

we conclude, using Theorem
5.2
.
2
, that the vertical morphisms induce an equivalence between
the cofibres of the horizontal maps. Therefore the square is a push-out square in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
.
We now use Theorem
5.2
.
3
in order to see that the lower-left and the upper-right corners are zero objects. The square therefore yields an equivalence

\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times X)\simeq\Sigma\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times X)\,.

By induction we now get (
5.4
).

∎

The following is Proposition
1.26
from the introduction. Recall the Definition
1.25
.
1

of
G
-proper objects in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

If
X
is in
G\mathbf{LCH}_{+}^{\mathrm{prop}}
and proper homotopy equivalent to
a finite
G
-CW complex with finite stabilizers, then

\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(X)
is
G
-proper.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}
is homotopy invariant by Theorem
5.2
.
1

we can assume that
X
is a
G
-CW complex with finite stabilizers.
We argue by a finite induction on the number of
G
-cells.
Assume that
X
is obtained from
X^{\prime}
by attaching a
G
-cell of the form
G/H\times D^{n}
, where
H
is a finite subgroup of
G
.
Then
X^{\prime}
is a split-closed subspace of
X
by Proposition
5.1
.
2
such that
X\setminus X^{\prime}\cong G/H\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
By Theorem
5.2
.
2
and (
5.4
) we have a fibre sequence

\Sigma^{-n}\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(G/H)\to\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(X)\to\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(X^{\prime})\,.

Since
\Sigma^{-n}\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(G/H)
is
G
-proper by definition and
\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(X^{\prime})
is
G
-proper by the induction hypothesis
we can conclude that
\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(X)
is
G
-proper, too.
∎

The following is Theorem
1.27
from the introduction.

Theorem 5.6
.

1.

If
P
is ind-
G
-proper, then the functor

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,-)\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\to\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}

sends all exact sequences to fibre sequences.

2.

If
P
is
G
-proper, then the functor

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,-)\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\to\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}

preserves filtered colimits.

Proof.

We first show Assertion
1
.
The full subcategory of
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
of objects
P
such that
\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,-)

sends all exact sequences in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
to fibre sequences is localizing.
In view of the Definition
1.25
.
2
of ind-
G
-properness it suffices to check that the functor

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(G/H),-)\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\to\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}

sends exact sequences in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
to fibre sequences for all finite subgroups
H
of
G
.

We will use that the usual
K
-theory functor for
C^{*}
-algebras (
1.12
) sends all exact sequences to fibre sequences.
By Corollary
1.24
we have the first equivalence in

{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}(C_{0}(G/H),{-})\simeq\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-)}\rtimes H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{erwfoijoiwefrweffwe}}}{{=}}K^{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}}({\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-)}\rtimes H)\,.

(5.5)

Since
{\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-)}\rtimes H
preserves exact sequences and
K^{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}}
sends
exact sequences in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
to fibre sequence we see that

{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}(C_{0}(G/H),{-})
sends exact sequences in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})
to fibre sequences.

The proof of Assertion
2
is completely analogous.
The full subcategory of
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
of objects
P

such that
\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,-)\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\to\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}
preserves filtered colimits is thick.
Since the functors
K^{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}}
and
{\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-)}\rtimes H
preserve filtered colimits we see that the category in question contains the algebras
C_{0}(G/H)
for all finite subgroups
H
of
G
. Consequently it contains
all
G
-proper objects.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.15
.

The proof is based on the defining relation

K^{G,\mathrm{an}}_{A}(X)\simeq\mathrm{KK}^{G}(\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(X),A)\,.

(5.6)

Assertion
1
is an immediate consequence of Proposition
1.20
.

Assertion
2
follows from Theorem
5.2
.
1
.

Assertion
3
follows
by applying the exact functor
\mathrm{KK}^{G}(-,A)
to the fibre sequence in Theorem
5.2
.
2

For Assertion
4
we consider the cases separately. If the sequence of algebras is semisplit, then
the assertion immediately follows from the fact that
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
is semiexact by Theorem
1.18
.
3
. If it is just exact, then we use Theorem
5.6
.
1
and Proposition

5.5
instead.
For the last statement note that if

X
is second countable, then
C_{0}(X)
is separable, and hence
\mathrm{kk}^{G}C_{0}(X)\simeq y^{G}(\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(C_{0}(X)))
is a compact object of
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
, see (
5.2
) and Remark
1.9
.

The first equivalence in Assertion
5
is an immediate consequence of Theorem
5.2
.
3
. The second equivalence uses Theorem
5.2
.
4

and the equivalence

K^{G,\mathrm{an}}_{A}(-)\simeq{\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}}(\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}C_{0}(-),\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(A))

for separable
A

as functors on
G\mathbf{LCH}_{\mathrm{2nd},+}^{\mathrm{prop}}
which follows from the fact that
y^{G}
in (
1.6
) is fully faithful.

In order to see Assertion
6
first note that for
Y
in
G\mathbf{LCH}_{\mathrm{2nd},+}^{\mathrm{prop}}
and
A
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})
we have an equivalence

K^{G,\mathrm{an}}_{A}(Y)\simeq{\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}}(\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(C_{0}(Y)),\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(A))\,.

We use this expression in terms of the mapping spectrum in order to make it obvious that a colimit in the first argument
can be pulled out as a limit.
We can express the intersection of the decreasing family of subspaces in categorical terms as a limit

\bigcap_{n\in{\rm Nature}}X_{n}\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}X_{n}

which is interpreted in
G\mathbf{LCH}_{+}^{\mathrm{prop}}
. By Gelfand duality we get

C_{0}\big(\bigcap_{n\in{\rm Nature}}X_{n}\big)\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}C_{0}(X_{n})\,,

where the colimit is taken in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})
.
We now show that the diagram
n\mapsto C_{0}(X_{n})
is admissible in the sense of
[
MN06
, Def. 2.5]
.
According to the criterion
[
MN06
, Lem. 2.7]
it suffices to construct a family of equivariant cpc maps
(s_{n}\colon C_{0}(\bigcap_{n\in{\rm Nature}}X_{n})\to C_{0}(X_{n}))_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
such that we have
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\to\infty}\iota_{n}\circ s_{n}={\mathtt{id}}_{C_{0}(\bigcap_{n\in{\rm Nature}}X_{n})}

in the norm topology, where
(\iota_{n}\colon C_{0}(X_{n})\to C_{0}(\bigcap_{n\in{\rm Nature}}X_{n}))_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
is the family of restriction maps.
By our assumption we can choose an equivariant cpc left-inverse
s_{0}\colon C_{0}(\bigcap_{n\in{\rm Nature}}X_{n})\to C_{0}(X_{0})
of the surjection
\iota_{0}
. Then we can define the sought equivariant cpc maps
s_{n}
as the composition of
s_{0}
with the restrictions
C_{0}(X_{0})\to C_{0}(X_{n})
. Since
\iota_{n}\circ s_{n}={\mathtt{id}}_{C_{0}(\bigcap_{n\in{\rm Nature}}X_{n})}
for every
n
in
{\rm Nature}
the family
(s_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
has the required property.
We finally use that

\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}
preserves filtered colimits of admissible diagrams by Theorem
1.4
.
7
.

The Assertion
7
follows from
Corollary
1.24

together
with (
5.5
).

Finally, Assertion
8
follows from Theorem
5.2
.
5
, Proposition
1.21
, and the general fact that for a stable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
the functor

{\mathtt{map}}\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbf{Sp}
has a natural lax symmetric monoidal refinement.
∎

6
Extension to
\bm{C^{*}}
-categories

In this section we consider the extension
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
(see Definition
1.29
) of the functor
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
to
C^{*}
-categories. Basic references for
C^{*}
-categories are
[
GLR85
]
,
[
Joa03
]
,
[
Mit02
]
,
[
Del12
]
,
[
AV20
]
.
We will in particular use the language introduced in
[
Bun19
]
,
[
Bun
]
which we recall in the following.

We start with the category
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of possibly non-unital
*
-categories which are
{\mathbb{C}}
-vector space enriched categories
{\mathbf{C}}
with an involution
*\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
which fixes objects and acts anti-linearly on morphisms. Morphisms in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
are functors which are compatible with the involution and the enrichment.
The category
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
contains the full subcategory
{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of
*
-algebras considered as categories with a single object.
Using the uniqueness of the norm on a
C^{*}
-algebra and the automatic continuity of
*
-homomorphisms between
C^{*}
-algebras we view the category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of
C^{*}
-algebras as a full subcategory of
{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
so that we can talk about functors (i.e. morphisms in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
) from
*
-categories to
C^{*}
-algebras.

Given a
*
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
we define a maximal norm on morphisms
by
\|f\|_{\max}\coloneqq\sup_{\rho}\|\rho(f)\|_{B}
, where the supremum runs over all functors
\rho
from
{\mathbf{C}}
to
C^{*}
-algebras
B
. This norm may be infinite.
We call
{\mathbf{C}}
a pre-
C^{*}
-category if all morphisms have a finite maximal norm.
In this way we get the full subcategory
{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of pre-
C^{*}
-categories. Its intersection with
{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is the category
{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of pre-
C^{*}
-algebras. A
C^{*}
-category is a pre-
C^{*}
-category in which all morphism spaces are complete with respect to the
maximal norm. As explained in
[
Bun19
]
,
[
Bun
]
this definition is equivalent to other definitions in the literature. Again, we get the category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of
C^{*}
-algebras by intersecting
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
with
{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

There is also a unital version of all the above. The corresponding categories will be denoted in the same way but without the superscript ’
\mathrm{nu}
’.

The categories explained above are connected by
adjunctions

\mathrm{compl}:{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\rightleftarrows C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{incl}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{incl}:{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\rightleftarrows{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{Bd}^{\infty}

(6.1)

constructed in
[
Bun
]
,
where
\mathrm{compl}
is the completion functor and the right adjoint

\mathrm{Bd}^{\infty}
is the bounded morphisms functor (since we only need the existence of
\mathrm{Bd}^{\infty}
we refrain from explaining it more precisely).
These adjunctions restrict correspondingly to adjunctions

\mathrm{compl}:{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\rightleftarrows C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{incl}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{incl}:{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\rightleftarrows{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{Bd}^{\infty}\,.

(6.2)

Using that
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is complete and cocomplete we can conclude formally from the existence of the
adjunctions (which are localizations or colocalizations, respectively) that all categories introduced above are complete and cocomplete, as well. We refer to
[
Bun
, Thm. 4.1]
for details.

While we define the extension
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
using the left adjoint functor
A^{f}
from the adjunction (
1.23
) the verification of most of the properties of
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}

uses another
C^{*}
-algebra
A({\mathbf{C}})
associated to a
C^{*}
-category.
The problem with
A(-)
is that it is only functorial for functors between
C^{*}
-categories which are injective on objects
so that
A(-)
can not directly be used to define
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
.

Construction 6.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. Following
[
Joa03
]
we can form
the object

A^{{\mathrm{alg}}}({\mathbf{C}})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{C,C^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{C}}}{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}(C,C^{\prime})

(6.3)

of
{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

with the obvious matrix multiplication and involution. If
{\mathbf{C}}
is in
{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, then

A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{C}})
actually belongs to
{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
so that we define the object of
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

A({\mathbf{C}})\coloneqq\mathrm{compl}(A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{C}}))

(6.4)

by applying the completion functor from (
6.2
).

The construction of
A({\mathbf{C}})
from
{\mathbf{C}}
is only functorial for functors in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
which are injective on objects. We therefore introduce the wide subcategory
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{inj}}
of
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of injective
functors and obtain a functor

A:C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{inj}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ .

(6.5)

We have a canonical isometric functor

{\mathbf{C}}\to A({\mathbf{C}})

(6.6)

which sends a morphism
f\colon C\to C^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{C}}
to the corresponding one-entry matrix
[f_{C^{\prime},C}]
.
As further shown in
[
Joa03
]
the functor
{\mathbf{C}}\to A({\mathbf{C}})
is initial for functors
\rho\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to B
with
B
a
C^{*}
-algebra
with the property that

\rho(f)\rho(f^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\rho(f\circ f^{\prime})&\text{if the composition is defined}\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}

(6.7)

for any morphisms
f,f^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{C}}
.

∎

Let
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a morphism in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{inj}}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is an isometric inclusion, then

A({\mathbf{C}})\to A({\mathbf{D}})
is an isometric inclusion.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
be a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{C}}
with finitely many objects, and let
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
be the
full subcategory of
{\mathbf{D}}
on the image of the objects of
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
.
As said above, for every object
C
in
{\mathbf{C}}
the map
{\mathtt{End}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}(C)\to A({\mathbf{C}})
is an isometry, and similarly for objects of
{\mathbf{D}}
.
This easily implies that the upper horizontal and the vertical maps in

\textstyle{A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{D}})}

are isometries.
Since
A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{C}})
is the union of the subalgebras of the form
A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})

we conclude that the maximal norm on this
*
-algebra is the norm induced from the representation on
A({\mathbf{D}})
.
This implies the assertion.
∎

Let
{\mathbf{C}}\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{inj}}}
be a diagram.

Lemma 6.3
.

If
I
is filtered, then the canonical map

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A({\mathbf{C}})\to A(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

As a formal consequence of the adjunctions in (
6.1
)
we have the following formula for colimits in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
:

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}\cong\mathrm{compl}({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}})\,.

(6.8)

Here

{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}}
stands for the colimit interpreted in the category
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. The latter happens to belong to
{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
so that we can apply the completion functor
\mathrm{compl}
.

We claim that for any
{\mathbf{D}}
in
{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
there is canonical isomorphism

A(\mathrm{compl}({\mathbf{D}}))\cong\mathrm{compl}(A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{D}}))\,.

(6.9)

In order to show the claim we
form the square

\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1)}
\scriptstyle{(2)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{compl}({\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(3)}
\textstyle{A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(4)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{compl}(A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{D}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A(\mathrm{compl}({\mathbf{D}}))}

The maps
(1)
and
(4)
are the canonical completion maps and the maps

(3)
and
(2)

are instances of (
6.6
). The dotted arrow is induced from the universal property of
(2)
applied to the composition
(3)\circ(1)
. Finally, the dashed map comes from the universal
property of
(4)
applied to the dotted arrow. This dashed arrow induces the desired isomorphism:
In order to construct an inverse we consider the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1)}
\scriptstyle{(2)}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{compl}({\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(3)}
\textstyle{A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(4)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{compl}(A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{D}}))}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces A(\mathrm{compl}({\mathbf{D}}))}

We get the dotted arrow from the universal property of
(1)
applied to
(4)\circ(2)
, and then the dashed arrow
from the universal property of
(3)
applied to the dotted arrow. It is straightforward to check that the dashed arrows in the two diagrams are inverse to each other.
This finishes the proof of the isomorphism (
6.9
).

Since taking objects is a left adjoint
[
Bun
, Lem. 2.4]
and therefore commutes with colimits we have a bijection

\mathrm{Ob}({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}})\cong{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{\mathbf{Set}}}\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{C}})\ .

Furthermore, if
C,C^{\prime}
are objects
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}
, then we can find
i
in
{\mathbf{I}}
and objects
\tilde{C},\tilde{C}^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{C}}(i)

such that
\iota_{i}(\tilde{C})=C
and
\iota_{i}(\tilde{C}^{\prime})=C^{\prime}
, where
\iota_{i}:{\mathbf{C}}(i)\to{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}}
is the canonical functor.
Then

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}}(C,C^{\prime})\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{(\kappa:i\to i^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{I}}_{i/}}{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{C}}(i^{\prime})}({\mathbf{C}}(\kappa)(\tilde{C}),{\mathbf{C}}(\kappa)(\tilde{C}^{\prime}))\,.

From this description and the formula (
6.3
) we easily conclude that

{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{C}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}})

(6.10)

is an isomorphism.
We get the isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A({\mathbf{C}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{erwgoijwioergewrgergwreg}}}{{\cong}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\mathrm{compl}(A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{C}}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\cong}}\mathrm{compl}({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}A^{{\mathrm{alg}}}({\mathbf{C}}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{wqef09uqj09efqwef}}}{{\cong}}\mathrm{compl}(A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{wregoijowergwergrf}}}{{\cong}}A(\mathrm{compl}({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{qwefqfjoiqfwefqwefeww}}}{{\cong}}A(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{{\mathbf{I}}}}{\mathbf{C}})\,,

where for the marked isomorphism we use that
\mathrm{compl}
and the
inclusion
\mathrm{incl}\colon{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
are left-adjoints (see (
6.2
)) and therefore commute with all colimits.
The colimits without superscripts are interpreted in
C^{*}
-algebras or
C^{*}
-categories, respectively.
∎

Recall Definition
1.30
of a separable
C^{*}
-category.
For every
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
we consider the inductive system
({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}{\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}{\mathbf{C}}}}
of invariant separable subcategories. Using that
G
is countable we have an isomorphism

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}{\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}{\mathbf{C}}}}{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\cong{\mathbf{C}}

(6.11)

Let
H
be a second group and
R\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{inj}}})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BH,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})

be a functor. For an invariant separable subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
of
{\mathbf{C}}
we let

R({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})^{R({\mathbf{C}})}
be the image of

R({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})\to R({\mathbf{C}})
. The following generalizes Definition
4.1
. Assume that
R
sends separable categories to separable algebras.

Definition 6.4
.

We say that
R
is
\mathrm{Ind}
-s-finitary if it has the following properties:

1.

For every
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
the inductive system
(R({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})^{R({\mathbf{C}})})_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}{\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}{\mathbf{C}}}}
is cofinal in the inductive system of all invariant separable
subcategories of
R({\mathbf{C}})
.

2.

The canonical map
(R({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}))_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}{\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}{\mathbf{C}}}}\to(R({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})^{R({\mathbf{C}})})_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}{\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}{\mathbf{C}}}}
is an isomorphism in

\mathrm{Ind}({\mathbf{Fun}}(BH,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}))
.

Note that Definition
1.31
of an
s
-finitary functor on
G
-
C^{*}
-categories makes sense for functors just defined on
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{inj}}})
.
If
R
is
\mathrm{Ind}
-s-finitary functor, then it is
s
-finitary. The converse of this statement is not true.

The Definition
6.4
is again designed in order to ensure the following fact analogous to Lemma
4.2
.

Lemma 6.5
.

If
F
is some
s
-finitary functor on
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BH,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
and
R
is
\mathrm{Ind}
-s-finitary, then the composition

F\circ R
is an
s
-finitary functor on
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{inj}}})
.

Lemma
4.3
has the following generalization for a
functor
R\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{inj}}})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BH,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Assume that
R
sends separable categories to separable algebras, satisfies the Condition
6.4
.
1
and one of the following:

1.

R
preserves countably filtered colimits.

2.

R
preserves isometric inclusions.

Then
R
is
\mathrm{Ind}
-s-finitary.

Proof.

The proof is completely analoguous to the proof of Lemma
4.3

taking advantage of the fact that Example
7.17

also shows that the poset invariant separable subcategories of
{\mathbf{C}}
in

{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
is countably filtered.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

The functors

A,A^{f}\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{inj}}})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
are
\mathrm{Ind}
-s-finitary.

Proof.

It follows from their constructions that the functors

A
and
A^{f}
send separable
G
-
C^{*}
-categories to separable
G
-
C^{*}
-algebras.

We now verify Condition
6.4
.
1
.
We consider the case of
A
. Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.
Let
B^{\prime}
be a
G
-invariant separable subalgebra of
A({\mathbf{C}})
. Let
\tilde{B}
be a countable dense subset of
B^{\prime}
.
For every
\tilde{b}
in
\tilde{B}
we choose a sequence
(\tilde{b}_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
in
A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{C}})
such that

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\to\infty}\tilde{b}_{n}=\tilde{b}
. The union of the
G
-orbits of the matrix elements of
\tilde{b}_{n}
for all
n
and
\tilde{b}
in
\tilde{B}
together generate a
G
-invariant separable subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. By construction we have
B^{\prime}\subseteq{A({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})^{A({\mathbf{C}})}}
.

The argument in the case of
A^{f}
is similar. We use that
A^{f}({\mathbf{C}})
the constructed as the closure of the free algebra

A^{f,\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{C}})
generated by the morphisms of
{\mathbf{C}}

subject to natural relations
[
Joa03
, Def. 3.7]
.
Let
B^{\prime}
be a separable subalgebra of
A^{f}({\mathbf{C}})
. Let
\tilde{B}
be a countable dense subset of
B^{\prime}
.
For every
\tilde{b}
in
\tilde{B}
we choose a sequence
(\tilde{b}_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
in
A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{C}})
such that
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\to\infty}\tilde{b}_{n}=\tilde{b}
.
We write
\tilde{b}_{n}
as a finite linear combination of finite products of morphisms from
{\mathbf{C}}
. This finite set of morphisms
will be called the set of components of
\tilde{b}_{n}
(it is irrelevant that this definition involves choices).
We let
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
be the
G
-invariant subcategory of
{\mathbf{C}}
generated by the union of
G
-orbits of the sets of components for all
n
in
{\rm Nature}
and
\tilde{b}
in
\tilde{B}
.
Then
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
is separable by construction and we have

B^{\prime}\subseteq{A^{f}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})^{A^{f}({\mathbf{C}})}}
.

We now employ Lemma
6.6
in order to finish the argument.
In the case of
A
we use that this functor preserves isometric inclusions by Lemma
6.2
.
In the case of
A^{f}
we use that this functor preserves all colimits since it is the left-adjoint of the adjunction in (
1.23
).
∎

The following corollary proves
Theorem
1.32
.
1
.

Corollary 6.8
.

The functor
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
is s-finitary.

Proof.

We combine the fact that
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
is
s
-finitary with
Lemma
6.5
and Lemma
6.7
for
A^{f}
.
∎

If

{\mathbf{C}}
is in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
, then the universal property of
A^{f}
being the left-adjoint in (
1.23
) applied to
{\mathbf{C}}\to A({\mathbf{C}})
from (
6.6
) provides the canonical morphism

\alpha_{{\mathbf{C}}}\colon A^{f}({\mathbf{C}})\to A({\mathbf{C}})

(6.12)

in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
such that

\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
(
6.6
)
\textstyle{A^{f}({\mathbf{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{{\mathbf{C}}}}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}})}

commutes. Here the left-down arrow is the unit of the adjunction (
1.23
). The family
\alpha=(\alpha_{{\mathbf{C}}})_{{\mathbf{C}}}
is a natural transformation of functors on
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathrm{inj}}})
.

The following proposition is the main technical result which makes all other arguments further below work.

Proposition 6.9
.

For every
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
the morphism

\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\alpha_{{\mathbf{C}}})\colon\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A^{f}({\mathbf{C}}))\to\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A({\mathbf{C}}))

is an equivalence.

Proof.

We first assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
is separable. In this case we can repeat the proof of
[
Joa03
, Prop. 3.8]
. We consider the separable
G
-Hilbert space
H\coloneqq L^{2}(\{{\mathbb{C}}\}\cup\mathrm{Ob}_{\not=0}({\mathbf{C}}))
, where
\mathrm{Ob}_{\not=0}({\mathbf{C}})
is the set of non-zero objects in
{\mathbf{C}}
and the
G
-action is induced by the action of
G
on the set of objects of
{\mathbf{C}}
. The additional
G
-fixed point
\{{\mathbb{C}}\}
induces an embedding
{\eta}\colon{\mathbb{C}}\to K(H)
.
The argument in the cited reference provides a morphism
\beta\colon A({\mathbf{C}})\to A^{f}({\mathbf{C}})\otimes K(H)
. It furthermore shows that
the composition

\beta\circ\alpha
(we omit the subscript
{\mathbf{C}}
for better readability) is homotopic to
{\mathtt{id}}_{A^{f}({\mathbf{C}})}\otimes\eta
, and that the composition

(\alpha\otimes{\mathtt{id}}_{K(H)})\circ\beta
is homotopic to
{\mathtt{id}}_{A({\mathbf{C}})}\otimes\eta
.
Using
\mathbb{K}^{G}
-stability and homotopy invariance of
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
we see that

\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\beta)\circ\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\alpha)\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{G}({{\mathtt{id}}_{A^{f}({\mathbf{C}})}\otimes\eta})
is an equivalence, and that

\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\alpha)\circ\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\beta)\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\alpha\otimes{\mathtt{id}}_{K(H)})\circ\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\beta)\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{G}({\mathtt{id}}_{A({\mathbf{C}})}\otimes\eta)
is an equivalence, too. Consequently,

\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\alpha)
is an equivalence.

We now drop the assumption that
{\mathbf{C}}
is separable. We then consider the poset
({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}{\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}{\mathbf{C}}}}
of invariant separable subcategories of
{\mathbf{C}}
. We have the following commutative diagram.

\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}{\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}{\mathbf{C}}}}\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A^{f}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}{\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}{\mathbf{C}}}}\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\alpha_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}{\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}{\mathbf{C}}}}\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A^{{f}}({\mathbf{C}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\alpha_{{\mathbf{C}}})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A({\mathbf{C}}))}

(6.13)

The upper horizontal arrow is an equivalence by the case discussed above.
The vertical morphisms are equivalences by a combination of Lemma
6.7
,
Lemma
6.5
and the fact that
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
is
s
-finitary.
We conclude that
\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\alpha_{{\mathbf{C}}})
is an equivalence.
∎

The following result will be used in
[
BEL
]
.
Note that if
{\mathbf{C}}
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is separable, then
A^{f}({\mathbf{C}})
and
A({\mathbf{C}})
are separable
C^{*}
-algebras by Lemma
6.7
.
Let
({\mathbf{C}}_{i})_{i\in I}
be a countable family of separable categories in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.

Lemma 6.10
.

We have a canonical equivalence

\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(A^{f}({\mathbf{C}}_{i}))\simeq\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(A^{f}(\coprod_{i\in I}{\mathbf{C}}_{i}))\,.

Proof.

Since
y^{G}:\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}
detects equivalences, Proposition
6.9

implies that for a separable
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
we have an equivalence

\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(\alpha_{{\mathbf{C}}}):\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(A^{f}({\mathbf{C}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(A({\mathbf{C}}))\ .

We use this for the equivalences marked by (1) in the following chain:

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(A^{f}({\mathbf{C}}_{i}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(A({\mathbf{C}}_{i}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(\bigoplus_{i\in I}A({\mathbf{C}}_{i}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(A(\coprod_{j\in I}{\mathbf{C}}_{i}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}(A^{f}(\coprod_{j\in I}{\mathbf{C}}_{i}))\,.

For
(2)
we use that
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}
preserves countable sums by Theorem
1.4
.
6
.
For the equivalence marked by
(3)
we use the fact that

A
sends coproducts of
C^{*}
-categories to direct sums of algebras.
This is immediate from the Construction
6.1
of
A
.
∎

Before we prove Theorem
1.32
.
3
, we first recall
the notion of a weak Morita equivalence from
[
BE
]
.
Let
{\mathbf{D}}
be in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and

S
be a subset of objects of
{\mathbf{D}}
.

Definition 6.11

(
[
BE
, Def. 18.1]
)
.

S
is weakly generating if
for every object
D
in
{\mathbf{D}}
, finite family

(A_{i})_{i\in I}
of morphisms
A_{i}\colon D_{i}\to D
in
{\mathbf{D}}
, and any
\epsilon
in
(0,\infty)

there exists a multiplier isometry
u\colon C\to D
such that
\|A_{i}-uu^{*}A_{i}\|\leq\epsilon
for all
i
in
I
and

C
is unitarily isomorphic in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{D}}
to a finite orthogonal sum in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{D}}

of objects in
S
.

Let
\phi\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a morphism in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.

Definition 6.12

(
[
BE
, Def. 18.3]
)
.

The functor

\phi
is called a weak Morita equivalence if:

1.

\phi
is fully faithfull,

2.

\phi(\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{C}}))
is weakly generating.

Note that being a weak Morita equivalence on depends on the underlying morphism between
C^{*}
-categories obtained by forgetting the
G
-action. As shown in the argument for
[
BE
, Cor. 18.7]
a unitary equivalence is a weak Morita equivalence.

Proposition 6.13
.

The functor
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
sends weak Morita equivalences to equivalences.

Proof.

Using the same method as at the end of the proof of
[
BE
, Thm. 18.6]
we can reduce to the case of weak Morita equivalences which are in addition
injective on objects. So from now one we assume that
\phi
is injective on objects.
We first assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
are separable.

By Proposition
6.9
and the fact that the image of
y^{G}
in (
1.6
) generates
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
, we see that it suffices to show that

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(A,A(\phi))\colon\mathrm{KK}^{G}(A,A({\mathbf{C}}))\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}(A,A({\mathbf{D}}))

(6.14)

is an equivalence for every
A
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})
. It furthermore suffices to show that this map induces an isomorphism on the level of homotopy groups.

Our assumptions imply that
A({\mathbf{C}})
and
A({\mathbf{D}})
belong to

{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})
.
By Proposition
1.20
it suffices to show that

K\!K^{G}_{*}(A,A(\phi))\colon K\!K^{G}_{*}(A,A({\mathbf{C}}))\to K\!K^{G}_{*}(A,A({\mathbf{D}}))

is an isomorphism.
In the proof of
[
BE
, Thm. 18.6]
we have constructed an equivariant Morita

(A({\mathbf{C}}),A({\mathbf{D}}))
-bimodule (the
G
