-action is induced by naturality of the construction) which induces the map
K\!K^{G}_{*}(A,A(\phi))
on the level of Kasparov modules. Since there exists an inverse Morita

(A({\mathbf{D}}),A({\mathbf{C}}))
-bimodule it is now clear that
K\!K^{G}_{*}(A,A(\phi))
is an isomorphism.
We conclude that
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(\phi)\colon\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}({\mathbf{D}})
is an equivalence if
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
are separable.

In order to extend to the general case we again
use that the functor
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
is s-finitary
by Corollary
6.8
.
Let
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
and
\phi:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a weak Morita equivalence which is also injective on objects.
In the following we show that we can obtain
\phi
as a colimit of weak Morita equivalences between cofinal families of separable invariant subcategories of
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
be invariant separable subcategories of
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
, respectively.
We choose a countable dense subset
M
of the morphisms of
{\mathbf{D}}
.
For every non-zero
D^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
and finite family
(A_{i})_{i}
with
A_{i}\colon{D_{i}^{\prime}\to D^{\prime}}
in
M
and
n
in
{\rm Nature}
we choose a finite family
(C_{j})_{j\in J}
of objects in
{\mathbf{C}}
and a multiplier isometry
u\colon\bigoplus_{j\in J}{\phi(C_{j})}\to D^{\prime}
(the sum is interpreted in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{D}}
)
such that
\|A_{i}-uu^{*}A_{i}\|\leq\frac{1}{n}
for all
i
in
I
. This is possible since
\phi
is a weak Morita equivalence.

We define the set objects of the invariant subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime\prime}
of
{\mathbf{C}}
as the set
of objects of
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
together with all
G
-orbits of the objects
C_{j}
appearing in these families above.
We then let
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime\prime}
be the smallest
G
-invariant
C^{*}
-subcategory of
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{D}}

containing
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
, the images of the objects of
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime\prime}
under
\phi
, the sums, their structure maps and the
u^{\prime}
s chosen above.
We furthermore let

{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime\prime}:=\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime\prime}\cap{\mathbf{D}}
. The subcategy
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime\prime}
is invariant and separable,
and we have
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime\prime}
.

Using that
\phi
is fully faithful we then define the morphisms of
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\phi
restricts to a fully faithful functor
\phi_{|{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime\prime}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime\prime}

which is a weak Morita equivalence.
The subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime\prime}
also invariant and separable and satisfies

{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime\prime}
.

Using these observations in a cofinality argument and the separable case already shown above we conclude that
the upper horizontal map in the following commutative square
is an eqivalence.

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 48.37212pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-48.37212pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 92.11697pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 92.11697pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern The colimits run over the posets of separable subcategories of
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
, respectively.
The vertical morphisms are equivalence since
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
is
s
-finitary by Corollary
6.8
.
It implies that the lower horizontal map is an equivalence.
∎

The following corollary verifies Theorem
1.32
.
2
.

Corollary 6.14
.

The functor
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
sends unitary equivalences to equivalences.

Proof.

We use that a unitary equivalence is a weak Morita equivalence
and apply Proposition
6.13
.
∎

Construction 6.15
.

We let
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
denote the subcategory of
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of unital
C^{*}
-categories
and unital functors, and then define the Dwyer–Kan localization

\ell\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\infty}

(6.15)

of
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
at the unitary equivalences.
As shown in
[
Del12
]
,
[
Bun19
]
the
\infty
-category
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\infty}
is complete and cocomplete and modelled by a combinatorial model category.
By
[
Lur09
, Sec. 4.2.4]
or
[
Cis19
, Prop. 7.9.2]
the functor

{\ell_{BG}:=\ell\circ-}\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\infty})

(6.16)

exhibits
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\infty})
as the Dwyer–Kan localization
of
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
again at the unitary equivalences.
Since the functor

\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
sends unitary equivalences to equivalences, we obtain a natural factorization

\textstyle{{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\ell_{BG}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\infty})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat},\infty}^{G}}

(6.17)

by the universal property of the Dwyer–Kan localization.

∎

The following proposition shows Theorem
1.32
.
4
.

Proposition 6.16
.

We have an equivalence

\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}(-\rtimes_{{?}}G)\simeq(-\rtimes_{{?}}G)\circ\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}

of functors from

{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})\to\mathrm{KK}
for
?\in\{r,\max\}
.

Proof.

We have the following chain of equivalences

\displaystyle\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}(-\rtimes_{?}G)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Def.\penalty\ }\ref{jgorijgqoiqfewfqwfewf}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{kk}(A^{f}(-\rtimes_{?}G))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Prop.\penalty\ }\ref{gihergioergregwefeerfwerf}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{kk}(A(-\rtimes_{?}G))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{kk}(A(-)\rtimes_{?}G)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqrwoigjweorgwerergerw}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle(-\rtimes_{?}G)\circ\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A(-))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Prop.\penalty\ }\ref{gihergioergregwefeerfwerf}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle(-\rtimes_{?}G)\circ\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A^{f}(-))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Def.\penalty\ }\ref{jgorijgqoiqfewfqwfewf}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(-\rtimes_{?}G)\circ\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(-)\,,

where for
!
we use the fact shown in
[
Bun
, Thm. 6.10]
that
A(-)
preserves the maximal crossed product, resp.
[
BE
, Thm. 12.23]
that
A(-)
preserves the reduced crossed product.
∎

An exact sequence
0\to{\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{B}}\to{\mathbf{C}}\to 0
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
is a
sequence of functors which induce bijections between the sets of objects and exact sequences

0\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{A}}}(C,C^{\prime})\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}(C,C^{\prime})\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}(C,C^{\prime})\to 0

for all pairs of objects
C,C^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{C}}
(which will be considered also as objects of
{\mathbf{A}}
and
{\mathbf{B}}
in the natural way). This is equivalent to the definition of an exact sequence of
G
-
C^{*}
-categories given in
[
Bun
, Def. 8.3]
.

The following proposition shows Theorem
1.32
.
5
and
1.32
.
6
.
Recall Definition
1.25
of
G
-properness and ind-
G
-properness.

Proposition 6.17
.

1.

If
P
in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
is ind-
G
-proper, then the functor
\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(-))

sends all exact sequences in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
to fibre sequences.

2.

If
P
in
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
is
G
-proper, then

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(-))
preserves filtered colimits.

Proof.

We start with Assertion
1
.
Let

0\to{\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{B}}\to{\mathbf{C}}\to 0

be an exact sequence in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.
Then by
[
Bun
, Prop. 8.9.2]
we have an exact sequence

0\to A({\mathbf{A}})\to A({\mathbf{B}})\to A({\mathbf{C}})\to 0

in

{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.
By Theorem
1.27
.
1
and the assumption on
P
we then get the fibre sequence

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,A({\mathbf{A}}))\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,A({\mathbf{B}}))\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,A({\mathbf{C}}))\,.

Finally, we get the desired fibre sequence

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}({\mathbf{A}}))\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}({\mathbf{B}}))\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}({\mathbf{C}}{))}

from Proposition
6.9
.

In order to show Assertion
2

we note that
A^{f}
preserves all colimits since it is a left adjoint. The assertion now immediately follows from Theorem
1.27
.
2
.
∎

In the following we need the notion of a
weakly invariant functors and of natural transformations between them from
[
Bun
, Def. 7.10]
. Furthermore we will use that the
orthogonal sum of two weakly invariant functors is again weakly invariant in a canonical way, see
[
BE
, Prop. 11.4.]
.
Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
. The following definition generalizes

[
BE
, Def. 11.3]
from the non-equivariant to the equivariant case.
Recall that
{\mathbf{C}}
is called additive if the underlying
C^{*}
-category obtained by forgetting the
G
-action admits orthogonal sums for all finite families of objects
[
BE
, Def. 5.5]
.

Definition 6.18
.

{\mathbf{C}}
is called flasque if it is additive and admits a weakly invariant endomorphism
S\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
such that we have a natural unitary isomorphism of weakly invariant functors
S\cong S\oplus{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}
.

The following proposition shows Assertion
7
of Theorem
1.32
.

Proposition 6.19
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
is flasque, then
\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}({\mathbf{C}}))\simeq 0
for all
ind-
G
-proper

P
in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
.

Proof.

Assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
is flasque.
The full subcategory of objects
P
in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
such that
\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}({\mathbf{C}}))\simeq 0
is localizing.
In view of Definition
1.25
it therefore suffices to show that

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(G/H),\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}({\mathbf{C}}))\simeq 0
for all finite subgroups
H
of
G
. In this case by Definition
1.29
, Proposition
6.9
and Corollary
1.24

we have the following equivalences (we omit
\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}
to simplify the notation):

\displaystyle\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(G/H),\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}({\mathbf{C}}))

\displaystyle{\simeq}

\displaystyle\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(G/H),A^{f}({\mathbf{C}}))

\displaystyle{\simeq}

\displaystyle\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(G/H),A({\mathbf{C}}))

\displaystyle{\simeq}

\displaystyle\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},A({\mathbf{C}})\rtimes H)\,.

We now use that
A(-)
commutes with crossed products
[
Bun
, Thm. 6.10]
and
again Proposition
6.9

(for the trivial group) and the definition of
K^{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}\coloneqq K\!K({\mathbb{C}},A^{f}(-))

in order to get an equivalence

K\!K({\mathbb{C}},A({\mathbf{C}})\rtimes H)\simeq K\!K({\mathbb{C}},A({\mathbf{C}}\rtimes H))\simeq K\!K({\mathbb{C}},A^{f}({\mathbf{C}}\rtimes H))\simeq K^{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}({\mathbf{C}}\rtimes H)\,.

We claim that
{\mathbf{C}}\rtimes H
is again flasque.
Let
S\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
be the weakly invariant functor implementing the flasqueness of
{\mathbf{C}}
such that we have a natural unitary
S\oplus{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}\cong S
of weakly invariant functors.
By
[
Bun
, Prop. 7.12]

the crossed product is functorial with respect to weakly invariant functors and unitary transformations between them. Hence
we get a unitary isomorphism

{S}\rtimes H\oplus{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}\rtimes H\cong({S\oplus{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}})\rtimes H\cong{S}\rtimes H\,.

Hence
S\rtimes H
implements the flasqueness of
{\mathbf{C}}\rtimes H
.
By
[
BE
, Prop. 13.13 & Thm. 14.4]
the functor

K^{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}
annihilates flasques so that

K^{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}({\mathbf{C}}\rtimes H)\simeq 0
. By going back through the equivalences above we conclude that

\mathrm{KK}^{G}({C_{0}(G/H)},\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}({\mathbf{C}}))\simeq 0
as desired.
∎

We finish the proof of Theorem
1.32

by showing Assertion
8
.
We consider a morphism
\phi\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
. Recall the notion
[
BE
, Def. 17.1]
of a relative Morita equivalence. Being a relative Morita equivalence is again a property of the underlying morphism obtained by forgetting the
G
-actions.

Proposition 6.20
.

If
P
in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
is ind-
G
-proper and

\phi\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a relative Morita equivalence, then

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(\phi))
is an equivalence.

Proof.

The argument is very similar to the proof of Proposition
6.19
.
The subcategory of all
P
in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
such that
\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(\phi))
is an equivalence is localizing. We then observe as in the proof of Proposition
6.19
that for any finite subgroup
H
of
G
the morphism

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(G/H),\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(\phi))
is equivalent to

K^{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}(\phi\rtimes H)
.

We then use that
-\rtimes H
preserves
relative Morita equivalences. Indeed,
-\rtimes_{r}H
preserves
relative Morita equivalences by
[
BE
, Prop. 17.2]

since the finite group
H
is exact, and furthermore
we have an isomorphism of functors
-\rtimes H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}-\rtimes_{r}H
.

We finally use that

K^{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}
sends relative Morita equivalences to equivalences by
[
BE
, Prop. 17.4]
.
∎

Recall the well-known result that the
K
-theory of stable multiplier algebras is trivial
[
Bla98
, Sec. 12]
. As an application of the techniques developed so far we will provide a generalization of this fact to the equivariant situation, see Corollary
6.22
below.

Let
H\coloneqq\ell^{2}\otimes L^{2}(G)
be the standard
G
-Hilbert space.
Let
A
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
. Then we consider the stable multiplier algebra
{\mathcal{M}}(A\otimes K(H))
of
A
as a
G
-
C^{*}
-category

\mathbf{M}(A\otimes K(H))

with a single object
[A\otimes K(H)]
.

Lemma 6.21
.

The category
{\mathbf{M}}(A\otimes K(H))
is flasque.

Proof.

We consider
A\otimes K(H)
as a
G
-
C^{*}
-category with a single object
[A\otimes K(H)]
.
We will show that
A\otimes K(H)
admits countable AV-sums
[
BE
, Def. 7.1]
.
Then
\mathbf{M}(A\otimes K(H))
is flasque by
[
BE
, Ex. 11.5]
.
For any countable set
I
we can choose
a pairwise orthogonal family of isometries
(u_{i})_{i\in I}
,
u_{i}\colon\ell^{2}\to\ell^{2}
,
such that
\sum_{i\in I}u_{i}u_{i}^{*}={\mathtt{id}}_{\ell^{2}}
in the strict topology of
B(\ell^{2})
which we interpret as the multiplier algebra of
K(\ell^{2})
.
We have an isomorphism
K(H)\cong K(\ell^{2})\otimes K(L^{2}(G))

and define the multiplier

e_{i}
in
{\mathcal{M}}(A\otimes K(H))
such that

e_{i}(a\otimes b\otimes c)\coloneqq a\otimes{u_{i}b}\otimes c
for every
a
in
A
,
b
in
K(\ell^{2})
and
c
in
K(L^{2}(G))
.
Then
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
is a mutually orthogonal family of isometries such that

\sum_{i\in I}e_{i}e_{i}^{*}=1_{{[A\otimes K(H)]}}
, where the sum converges strictly.
According to
[
BE
, Prop. 7.1]
the pair
({[A\otimes K(H)]},(e_{i})_{i\in I})
represents the orthogonal sum of the family
({[A\otimes K(H)]})_{I}
in the
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{M}}(A\otimes K(H))
. The latter is therefore countably additive.
∎

Combining Lemma
6.21
with Proposition
6.19
immediately implies:

Corollary 6.22
.

We have

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(P,{\mathcal{M}}(A\otimes K(H)))\simeq 0

for all ind-
G
-proper
P
in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
.

If
G
is trivial and
P=\mathrm{kk}({\mathbb{C}})
, then this is, as already noted above, the well-known result that the
K
-theory of stable multiplier algebras is trivial. The classical proof is different and shows that the unitary group of such an algebra is contractible.

7
Tensor products of
\bm{C^{*}}
-categories

The main goal of the present section is to prove Theorem
1.35
from the introduction stating that

\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
has symmetric monoidal refinements for the maximal and minimal tensor products on
C^{*}
-categories.
In order to show this result we give an essentially complete account for the maximal and minimal tensor products on
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. As an easy consequence of the definitions in Corollary
7.8
we obtain an op-lax symmetric
monoidal refinement of
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
in both cases. So the main (and most complicated part) is the verification, stated as Proposition
7.9
, that this structure
is actually symmetric monoidal. The crucial
technical result used in its proof is the Proposition
7.10
asserting the compatibility of
A
from (
6.4
) with the minimal and maximal tensor products of
C^{*}
-categories.

Our starting point is the symmetric monoidal structure on
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
given by the algebraic tensor product.

Definition 7.1
.

For
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
the algebraic tensor product is characterized by the property that the morphism

{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}

in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
(possibly non-unital categories with involution) is universal for morphisms from

{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{D}}
to objects from
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
which are bilinear on morphism spaces.

Here is an
description of the algebraic tensor product of

{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
:

1.

objects: We have
\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}})\cong\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{C}})\times\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{D}})
.

2.

morphisms: For objects
(C,D)
and
(C^{\prime},D^{\prime})
in
{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}
we have

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}}((C,D),(C^{\prime},D^{\prime}))\cong{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}(C,C^{\prime}){\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}}{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(D,D^{\prime})\,.

3.

composition and involution: These structures are defined in the obvious manner.

The maximal tensor product in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
or
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
has a similar description by a universal property:

Definition 7.2
.

For
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
the maximal tensor product is characterized by the property that the morphism

{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{D}}

in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is universal for morphisms from

{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{D}}
to objects from
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
which are bilinear on morphism spaces.

One must check that the maximal tensor product exists. In the unital case this has been shown in
[
Del12
, Prop. 3.12]
, but the proof given there explicitly uses identity morphisms and does not directly apply in the non-unital case.
The first step in the verification is the following lemma.
Assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
,
{\mathbf{D}}
and
{\mathbf{E}}
are in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

The algebraic tensor products
{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}
and

{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{E}}
are pre-
C^{*}
-categories.

Proof.

It suffices to check that for morphisms

f
in
{\mathbf{C}}
and
g
in
{\mathbf{D}}
or
\phi
in
{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}
and
h
in
{\mathbf{E}}
the morphisms

f\otimes g
in
{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}
or
\phi\otimes h
in
{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{E}}
have finite maximal norms.
We will show that

\|f\otimes g\|_{\max}\leq\|f\|_{{\mathbf{C}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{D}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|\phi\otimes h\|\leq\|\phi\|_{\max}\|h\|_{{\mathbf{E}}}\,.

(7.1)

Let
\rho\colon{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}\to A
be a functor to a
C^{*}
-algebra (considered as a morphism in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
). We will show that
\|\rho(f\otimes g)\|_{A}\leq\|f\|_{{\mathbf{C}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{D}}}
. This fact is well-known for homomorphisms from algebraic tensor products on
C^{*}
-algebras
[
Mur90
, Cor. 6.3.6]
, see also Remark
7.4
below. Using the
C^{*}
-equality for the norm on
C^{*}
-categories, the case of
C^{*}
-categories can be reduced to the case of
C^{*}
-algebras as follows.
We have

\|\rho(f\otimes g)\|_{A}^{2}=\|\rho(f^{*}\otimes g^{*})\rho(f\otimes g)\|_{A}=\|\rho(f^{*}f\otimes g^{*}g)\|_{A}\leq\|f^{*}f\|_{{\mathbf{C}}}\|g^{*}g\|_{{\mathbf{D}}}=\|f\|_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{2}\|g\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{D}}}\,,

where for the inequality we use that
\rho
induces a representation of the algebraic tensor product of
C^{*}
-algebras
{\mathtt{End}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}(C)\otimes_{{{\mathbb{C}}}}^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathtt{End}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(D)
to
A
.
Since
\rho
is arbitrary the first inequality (
7.1
) follows.

In order to show the second inequality we argue similarly
using the corresponding fact for
C^{*}
-algebras: If
A,B,C
are in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
,
\psi
is in
A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B
and
c
is in
C
, then
for every representation
\rho\colon A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C\to D
with
D
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

we have

\|\rho(\psi\otimes c)\|_{D}\leq\|\psi\|_{\max}\|c\|_{C}\,,

(7.2)

see Remark
7.4
.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

The estimate (
7.2
) is clearly well-known, but since
we do not know any reference we provide the argument in some detail.
We consider
A,B,C,D
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and a homomorphism

\rho\colon A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C\to D
.
For
\psi
in
A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B
and
c
in
C
we then want to show that

\|\rho(\psi\otimes c)\|_{D}\leq\|\psi\|_{\max}\|c\|_{C}
.

In a first step, after replacing
D
by a subalgebra, we can assume that

\rho
has dense range.
In the following we construct homomorphisms

\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}\colon A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B\to{\mathcal{M}}(D)\,,\quad\rho_{B\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C}\colon B\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C\to{\mathcal{M}}(D)\,,\quad\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C}\colon A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C\to{\mathcal{M}}(D)

such that

\rho(aa^{\prime}\otimes bb^{\prime}\otimes cc^{\prime})=\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}(a\otimes b)\rho_{B\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C}(b^{\prime}\otimes c)\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C}(a^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime})

(7.3)

for all
a,a^{\prime}
in
A
, all
b,b^{\prime}
in
B
and all
c,c^{\prime}
in
C
. We discuss the construction of

\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}
in detail. The other two cases are analogous.
We identify elements in the multiplier algebra
{\mathcal{M}}(D)
with pairs
(l,r)
of maps

l,r\colon D\to D
satisfying the multiplier identity
r(d)d^{\prime}=dl(d^{\prime})
. For
a
in
A
and
b
in
B
with thus have to construct
the pair

\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}(a\otimes b)=(\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}^{L}(a\otimes b),\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}^{R}(a\otimes b))\,.

We explain the construction of
\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}^{L}(a\otimes b)
.
It is based on the following diagram:

\textstyle{{\mathtt{ker}}(\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime}\mapsto\rho(aa^{\prime}\otimes bb^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{\rho(A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{incl}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{L}_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}(a\otimes b)}

(7.4)

We start from the bold part. In a first step we show that the upper dotted arrow is zero. This implies
the existence of the lower dotted linear map. The argument will furthermore provide an estimate
showing that the latter is continuous. We then get the dashed arrow by continuous extension since
\mathrm{incl}
has dense range by our first reduction step.

Here are the details. For
a^{\prime}
in
A
and
b^{\prime}
in
B
we consider the linear map

\rho_{a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime}}\colon C\to D
defined by
c\mapsto a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime}\otimes c
. If
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
are positive elements, then

\rho_{a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime}}
is a positive map between
C^{*}
-algebras and hence continuous. General elements
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
can be written as finite linear combinations of positive elements. We conclude that
\rho_{a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime}}
is continuous in general.

We now consider
t\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\otimes c_{i}
in
A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C
and let

(w)
denote a normalized approximate unit of
C
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{w}\rho_{a\otimes b}(w)\rho(t)=\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{w}\rho(a\otimes b\otimes w)\rho(t)=\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{w}\rho\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}aa_{i}\otimes bb_{i}\otimes wc_{i}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{w}\rho(aa_{i}\otimes bb_{i}\otimes wc_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{w}\rho_{aa_{i}\otimes bb_{i}}(wc_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{aa_{i}\otimes bb_{i}}(c_{i})

\displaystyle=\rho\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}aa_{i}\otimes bb_{i}\otimes c_{i}\big)

using the continuity of
\rho_{aa_{i}\otimes bb_{i}}
at the marked equality.
This equality first of all implies that if
\rho(t)=0
, then
\rho(\sum_{i=1}^{n}aa_{i}\otimes bb_{i}\otimes c_{i})=0
, i.e., that
the upper dotted arrow in (
7.4
) vanishes.
It further implies the estimate

\rho\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}aa_{i}\otimes bb_{i}\otimes c_{i}\big)\leq\|\rho_{a\otimes b}\|\|\rho(t)\|\,,

hence the continuity of the lower dotted arrow in (
7.4
).

One constructs
\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}^{R}(a\otimes b)
in a similar manner.
The multiplier identity

\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}^{R}(a\otimes b)(d)d^{\prime}=d\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}^{L}(a\otimes b)(d^{\prime})

is easy to check for

d,d^{\prime}
in
\rho(A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C)
and extends by continuity to all of
D
.

In order to check (
7.3
) one observes that this equality holds if one multiplies it from the right or left by an element of

\rho(A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C)
.

In order to derive the estimate (
7.2
) we choose normalized approximate units

(u)
of
A
and

(v)
of
B
. We write
\psi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}
and calculate

\displaystyle\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{u}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{v}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{w}\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}(\psi)\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C}(u\otimes c)\rho_{B\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}C}(v\otimes w)=\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{u}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{v}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{w}\rho\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}u\otimes b_{i}v\otimes cw\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{u}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{v}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{w}\rho_{a_{i}u\otimes b_{i}v}(cw)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{u}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{v}\rho_{a_{i}u\otimes b_{i}v}(c)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{u}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{v}\rho_{a_{i}u\otimes c}(b_{i}v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{u}\rho_{a_{i}u\otimes c}(b_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{u}\rho_{b_{i}\otimes c}(a_{i}u)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{b_{i}\otimes c}(a_{i})=\rho\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\otimes c\big)

\displaystyle=\rho(\psi\otimes c)\,.

This gives

\|\rho(\psi\otimes c)\|_{D}\leq\|\rho_{A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B}(\psi)\|_{{\mathcal{M}}(D)}\|c\|_{C}\leq\|\psi\|_{\max}\|c\|_{C}

as desired.

∎

Proposition 7.5
.

The maximal tensor product
\otimes_{\max}
on
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
exists and equips this category with a symmetric monoidal structure.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
7.3

the algebraic tensor product induces a symmetric monoidal functor

C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{}_{\mathrm{pre}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. Using the completion functor we
define

{\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{{\max}}{\mathbf{D}}\coloneqq\mathrm{compl}({\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}})\,.

It remains to define the unit, associativity and symmetry constraints. Thereby only the associativity is not completely straightforward.
In order to construct it we consider the bold part of the commutative diagram

\textstyle{({\mathbf{A}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{B}})\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{B}})\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{B}})\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\max}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{C}})}

whose vertical morphisms are all given by the unit of the first adjunction in (
6.1
) and the functoriality of the algebraic tensor product. The upper horizontal functor is the associativity constraint of the algebraic tensor product.
We obtain
the dotted arrow from the universal property of the algebraic tensor product:
To this end we must show that the bilinear functor

({\mathbf{A}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{B}})\times^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\max}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{C}})

induced by the right-down composition extends by continuity to a bilinear functor

({\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{B}})\times^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\max}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{C}})\,.

For a morphism
\phi
in
{\mathbf{A}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{B}}
and
h
in
{\mathbf{C}}
we have by the second inequality in (
7.1
) that

\|\phi\otimes h\|_{{\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\max}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{C}})}\leq\|\phi\|_{\max}\|h\|_{{\mathbf{C}}}\,.

This estimate implies that the bilinear functor extends as desired, and the existence of the dotted arrow follows.

We finally get the dashed arrow from the universal property of the lower left vertical arrow applied to the dotted arrow.
In order to show that it is an isomorphism we construct an inverse by a similar argument starting from the inverse of
the upper horizontal arrow.
∎

It is clear from the universal property of
\otimes_{\max}
, or alternatively from its construction, that the inclusion functor

\mathrm{incl}\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}

has a canonical symmetric monoidal refinement for the maximal tensor structures on the domain and the target.

We now turn to the minimal tensor product on
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

The category
\mathbf{Hilb}
of small Hilbert spaces is a commutative algebra in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
such that the structure morphism

\mathbf{Hilb}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{Hilb}\to\mathbf{Hilb}

is induced by the universal property of
\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}
by the functor
\mathbf{Hilb}\times\mathbf{Hilb}\to\mathbf{Hilb}
given as follows:

1.

objects: A pair
(H,H^{\prime})
of Hilbert spaces is sent to
H\otimes H^{\prime}
(tensor product in the sense of Hilbert spaces).

2.

morphisms: A pair of morphism
(f,g)\colon(H_{0},H_{0}^{\prime})\to(H_{1},H_{1}^{\prime})
is sent to the morphism
f\otimes g\colon H_{0}\otimes H_{0}^{\prime}\to H_{1}\otimes H_{1}^{\prime}
.

The unit of this algebra is the inclusion functor
{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbf{Hilb}
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{D}}
be in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and

c\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to\mathbf{Hilb}
and
d\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to\mathbf{Hilb}
be functors. Then we can define
a functor

c\otimes d\colon{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}\to\mathbf{Hilb}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{Hilb}\to\mathbf{Hilb}\,.

Definition 7.6
.

The minimal tensor product
{\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{\min}{\mathbf{D}}
is defined as the completion of the algebraic tensor product such that
for every
c,d
as above we have a factorization

\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\otimes d}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Hilb}\,.}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{\min}{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

In other words,
the minimal norm of a morphism
\phi
in
{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}
is given by

\|\phi\|_{\min}\coloneqq{\sup}_{c,d}\,\|(c\otimes d)(\phi)\|_{\mathbf{Hilb}}\,.

(7.5)

Proposition 7.7
.

The minimal tensor product
\otimes_{\min}
equips
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
with a symmetric monoidal structure.

Proof.

We must provide the unit, associativity, and symmetry constraints.
As in the case of the maximal tensor product only the associativity constraint is non-straightforward.
In order to construct it we consider the bold part of the commutative diagram

\textstyle{({\mathbf{A}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{B}})\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\min}{\mathbf{B}})\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\min}{\mathbf{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\min}{\mathbf{B}})\otimes_{\min}{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\min}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\min}{\mathbf{C}})}

where the vertical maps are given by the canonical maps from the algebraic tensor products to the respective completions.

As in the case of the maximal tensor product, in order to show the existence
of the dotted arrow we must show that the bilinear functor

({\mathbf{A}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{B}})\times^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\min}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\min}{\mathbf{C}})

induced by the right-down composition extends by continuity to a bilinear functor

({\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\min}{\mathbf{B}})\times^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\min}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\min}{\mathbf{C}})\,.

Let
a\colon{\mathbf{A}}\to\mathbf{Hilb}
,
b\colon{\mathbf{B}}\to\mathbf{Hilb}
and
c\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to\mathbf{Hilb}
be representations.
Let
\phi
be in
({\mathbf{A}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{B}})
and
h
be in
{\mathbf{C}}
.
Then we have the inequalities

\|(a\otimes b\otimes c)(\phi\otimes h)\|_{\mathbf{Hilb}}\leq\|(a\otimes b){(\phi)}\|_{\mathbf{Hilb}}\|c(h)\|_{\mathbf{Hilb}}\leq\|{\phi}\|_{{\min}}\|h\|_{{\mathbf{C}}}\,.

Since
a,b,c
are arbitrary we conclude that

\|\phi\otimes h\|_{{\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\min}({\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\min}{\mathbf{C}})}\leq\|\phi\|_{\min}\|h\|_{{\mathbf{C}}}
.
This estimate implies that the bilinear functor extends as desired and that the dotted arrow exists.

The first part of the estimate above shows that the dotted arrow further extends by continuity to the dashed arrow.
An inverse of the dashed arrow can be constructed in a similar manner starting from the inverse of the upper horizontal arrow.
∎

It is again clear from the universal property of
\otimes_{\min}
, or alternatively from the construction of the minimal norm in (
7.5
),
that the inclusion functor

\mathrm{incl}\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}

has a canonical symmetric monoidal refinement for the minimal tensor structures on the domain and the target.

Let us now collect some facts about the minimal tensor product which we will use at various places in the present section.

If
A
is in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, then a representation
\alpha\colon A\to\mathbf{Hilb}
of
A
is the same datum as a homomorphism
\alpha\colon A\to B(H)
for some Hilbert space
H
. If
\beta\colon B\to B(H^{\prime})
is a second homomorphism, then their tensor product in the sense of representations to
\mathbf{Hilb}
is simply
the tensor product

\alpha\otimes\beta\colon A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B\to B(H)\otimes^{{\mathrm{alg}}}B(H^{\prime})\to B(H\otimes H^{\prime})\,.

It is known that if
\alpha
and
\beta
are faithful representations, then

\|x\|_{\min}=\|(\alpha\otimes\beta)(x)\|_{B(H\otimes H^{\prime})}

(7.6)

for all
x
in
A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B
.
Thus for
C^{*}
-algebras the supremum in
(
7.5
) is realized by any pair of faithful representations.

Corollary 7.8
.

The functor
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
canonically refines to an op-lax symmetric monoidal functor

{\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G,{\otimes}_{?}}}\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu},\otimes_{?}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G,{\otimes}_{?}}

for
?\in\{\min,\max\}
.

Proof.

As observed previously, the inclusion functor in (
1.23
) has a symmetric monoidal refinement for the structures
\otimes_{?}
. Hence its left-adjoint

A^{f}
acquires a canonical op-lax symmetric monoidal structure. Since
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
is symmetric monoidal by Proposition
1.21
we conclude that the composition
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}=\mathrm{kk}^{G}\circ A^{f}
has a canonical op-lax symmetric monoidal structure.
∎

The following proposition finishes the verification of Theorem
1.35
from the introduction.

Proposition 7.9
.

For
?\in\{\min,\max\}
the op-lax symmetric monoidal functor

{\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G,{\otimes}_{?}}}\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu},\otimes_{?}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G,{\otimes}_{?}}

is symmetric monoidal.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
,
{\mathbf{D}}
be in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. Then
we have functors
{\mathbf{C}}\to A({\mathbf{C}})
and
{\mathbf{D}}\to A({\mathbf{D}})
.
We consider the composition

{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\to A({\mathbf{C}})\times A({\mathbf{D}})\to A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes_{?}A({\mathbf{D}})

in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
This functor is bilinear and hence, by the universal property of the respective tensor products, factorises uniquely over the functor

i
in

\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{?}{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
(
6.6
)
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes_{?}A({\mathbf{D}})\,.}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{?}{\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We now use the universal property of the functor
A

formulated in Construction
6.1
(the conditions are straightforward to check), that the functor
i
further factorizes over the dashed homomorphism as indicated. We will call this the canonical homomorphism in what follows.

Proposition 7.10
.

For all
{\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{D}}
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and
?
in
\{\min,\max\}
the
canonical homomorphism

A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{?}{\mathbf{D}})\to A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes_{?}{A}({\mathbf{D}})

(7.7)

is an isomorphism

Remark 7.11
.

One can use Proposition

in the case
?=\max
in order to show that the definition of the maximal tensor product of
C^{*}
-categories given in
[
AV20
, Sec. 3.1]
is equivalent to the Definition
7.2
used in the present paper.

∎

.

)

Using Proposition
6.9
for the left vertical arrow and Proposition
1.21
for the equivalence

in order to deal with the right vertical arrow, we conclude that the vertical arrows are equivalences. We conclude that

The following lemma is the first step of the proof of Proposition
7.10
.

Lemma 7.12
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
,
{\mathbf{D}}
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
have at most finitely many objects, then the canonical homomorphism

A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{?}{\mathbf{D}})\to A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes_{?}A({\mathbf{D}})
is an isomorphism for
?
in
\{\min,\max\}
.

Proof.

The assumption implies that
A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{C}})\to A({\mathbf{C}})
and
A^{\mathrm{alg}}({\mathbf{D}})\to A({\mathbf{D}})
are isomorphisms,
where
A^{\mathrm{alg}}
is as in (
6.3
). Since the algebraic tensor product in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is formed
on the level of underlying complex vector spaces and direct sums commute with tensor products we furthermore conclude that
the algebraic analog

A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}})\to A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}A({\mathbf{D}})

of the canonical homomorphism is an isomorphism.
We now consider the diagram

\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{?}{\mathbf{D}})}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}A({\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes_{?}A({\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

If
?=\max
, then we obtain the dotted arrow from the universal property of the lower horizontal arrow applied to the up-right composition.

In the case of
?=\min
we argue as follows. In order to show that the dotted arrow exists, by the universal property of
\otimes_{\min}
on
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
we must show that for every pair of representations
c\colon A({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathbf{Hilb}
and
d\colon A({\mathbf{D}})\to\mathbf{Hilb}
we have a factorization as indicated by the dashed arrow in the extended diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{?}{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c^{\prime}\otimes d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{!}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}{\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{?}{\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Hilb}\,.}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}A({\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes_{?}A({\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\otimes d}

To this end we consider the dotted part of the diagram, where
c^{\prime}\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to\mathbf{Hilb}
and
d^{\prime}\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to\mathbf{Hilb}
are the restrictions of
c
and
d
along
{\mathbf{C}}\to A({\mathbf{C}})
and
{\mathbf{D}}\to A({\mathbf{D}})
.
The arrow
c^{\prime}\otimes d^{\prime}
exists by the universal property of the minimal tensor product on
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
We get the dashed arrow from the universal property of the arrow marked by
!
.

In order to see in both cases of ? that the homomorphism

A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes_{?}A({\mathbf{D}})\to A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{?}{\mathbf{D}})
just constructed
is inverse to the canonical homomorphism
A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{?}{\mathbf{D}})\to A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes_{?}A({\mathbf{D}})
one observes that this is the case by construction
after restriction to the algebraic tensor products.
∎

In order to show Proposition
7.10
in general
we must extend Lemma
7.12
from
C^{*}
-categories with finitely may objects to arbitrary
C^{*}
-categories. Our argument for this will depend on the following lemma which already has been used earlier in the proof of Proposition
3.8
.

Lemma 7.13
.

1.

The functor
\otimes_{\max}
on
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves filtered colimits in each argument.

2.

The functor
\otimes_{\min}
on
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves filtered colimits in each argument whose structure maps are isometric inclusions.

The Assertion
7.13
.
1
seems to be well-known
[
Bla06
, II.9.6.5]
, but we could not find a detailed proof.
We will show both assertions besides some other interesting results about tensor products of
C^{*}
-categories below.

Proof of Proposition
7.10
assuming Lemma
7.13
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, then we have a canonical isomorphism

{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}\,{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathbf{C}}\,,

where the colimit runs over the full subcategories of
{\mathbf{C}}
with finitely many objects
10
10

10

we really mean subcategories with finitely many objects in contrast to separable subcategories as considered in other places
.
The structure maps of this system are fully faithful functors which are injective on objects.
By Lemma
6.3
we therefore get an isomorphism

{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}\,A({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}A({\mathbf{C}})\,,

(7.9)

where the structure map of the system of
A({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})
are isometric inclusions.
We now consider the diagram

\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}}A({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})\otimes_{?}A({\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
Lem.
7.12
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}}A({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\otimes_{?}{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes_{?}A({\mathbf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{?}{\mathbf{D}})}

where the left vertical isomorphism uses Lemma
7.13
and (
7.9
) (also for
{\mathbf{D}}
),
and the right vertical arrow exists by the universal property of the colimit.
One checks that the dotted arrow defined by this square is inverse to
the canonical homomorphism since this is true
after restricting to the algebraic tensor products for the uncompleted version
A^{\mathrm{alg}}
of the functor
A
.
∎

In the following discussion we show a couple of results which prepare the actual proof of Lemma
7.13
.
At the end we use the material in order to derive some additional results which will be used, e.g. in
[
BEL
]
.
Our presentation will be selfcontained with one exception: the exactness of the maximal tensor product for
C^{*}
-algebras
[
BO08
, Prop. 3.7.1]
, but this does not go into the proof of Lemma
7.13
.

We start with an explicit model for filtered colimits in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
We consider a small filtered category
{\mathbf{I}}
and a functor
{\mathbf{C}}\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
In the following construction together with Proposition
7.15
we provide an explicit model for the
C^{*}
-category
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}
.

Construction 7.14
.

The colimit
{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}}
of the image of the diagram in
{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}
has the following explicit description. For every
i
in
{\mathbf{I}}
we let
\iota_{i}\colon{\mathbf{C}}_{i}\to{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}}
denote the canonical map.

1.

objects: The set objects of
{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}}
is given by
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{C}})
.

2.

morphisms: For every two objects
\bar{C}
and
\bar{C}^{\prime}
in
{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}}
we can (since
{\mathbf{I}}
is filtered) find
i
in
{\mathbf{I}}
and objects
C
and
C^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{C}}_{i}
such that

\bar{C}=\iota_{i}(C)
and
\bar{C}^{\prime}=\iota_{i}(C^{\prime})
. We then have

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}}}(\bar{C},\bar{C}^{\prime})\coloneqq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{(i\to i^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{I}}_{i/}}{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{i^{\prime}}}({\mathbf{C}}(i\to i^{\prime})(C),{\mathbf{C}}(i\to i^{\prime})(C^{\prime}))\,,

(7.10)

where
{\mathbf{I}}_{i/}
denotes the slice category of objects under
i
in
{\mathbf{I}}
, and the colimit is taken in
{\mathbf{Vect}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
.

3.

composition and involution: These structures are defined in the canonical manner.

We now define a norm on
{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}}
as follows.
If
\bar{f}\colon\bar{C}\to\bar{C}^{\prime}
is any morphism in
{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}{\mathbf{C}}
, then there exists
i
and
C,C^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{C}}_{i}
as in Point
2
above and

(i\to i^{\prime})
in
{\mathbf{I}}_{i/}
and a morphism
f
in
{\mathbf{C}}_{i^{\prime}}
such that
\iota_{i^{\prime}}(f)=\bar{f}
.
We then define

\|\bar{f}\|\coloneqq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{(\phi\colon i^{\prime}\to i^{\prime\prime})\in{\mathbf{I}}_{i^{\prime}\!/}}\|\phi(f)\|_{C_{i^{\prime\prime}}}\,.

Since the map

(\phi\colon i^{\prime}\to i^{\prime\prime})\mapsto\|\phi(f)\|_{C_{i^{\prime\prime}}}
is decreasing and bounded below by
0
the limit exists.
Since
{\mathbf{I}}
is filtered the right-hand side does not depend on the choices of
i^{\prime}
and
f
.

We form the completion

{\mathbf{D}}\coloneqq\overline{{{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}}{\mathbf{C}}}

with respect to the norm defined above. This amounts to forming the completion of the morphism spaces and extending the composition and the involution by continuity.
Note that this process also involves forming the quotient by the subcategory of morphisms with zero norm, and hence the map from the original category to its completion is not necessarily injective.
Since the norm in
{\mathbf{C}}_{i}
satisfies the
C^{*}
-equality and inequality for every
i
in
{\mathbf{I}}
we conclude from the construction that also the norm on
{\mathbf{D}}
has these properties. Consequently,
{\mathbf{D}}
is an object in
{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}}
.

The family of structure maps
(\iota_{i})_{i\in{\mathbf{I}}}
provides the first map of the composition

{\mathbf{C}}\to\underline{{{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}}{\mathbf{C}}}\to\underline{{\mathbf{D}}}

(7.11)

in
{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathbf{I}},{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}})
, where
\underline{\smash{-}}
stands for forming the constant
{\mathbf{I}}
-diagram on
-
.
The second morphism is induced by the inclusion of the colimit into its completion.
Since the inclusion functor
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
is fully faithful, the inclusion (
7.11
) is a morphism

{\mathbf{C}}\to\underline{{\mathbf{D}}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathbf{I}},{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}})
, and hence by adjunction corresponds a functor

\sigma\colon\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}\,.

(7.12)

The following proposition shows that
{\mathbf{D}}
is an explicit model for the
C^{*}
-category
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}
.

∎

Proposition 7.15
.

The functor
\sigma
from (
7.12
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We construct an inverse.
We have
a canonical functor

\kappa\colon{{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{nu}}}}{\mathbf{C}}\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}

which in view of the formula (
6.8
) is just the completion map. Using the notation from Point
7.14
.
2
we have

\|\kappa(\bar{f})\|_{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}}=\|\kappa\iota_{i^{\prime\prime}}\phi(f)\|_{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}}\leq\|\phi(f)\|_{C_{i^{\prime\prime}}}

for all
(\phi\colon i^{\prime}\to i^{\prime\prime})\in{\mathbf{I}}_{i^{\prime}\!/}
. By considering the limit over
{\mathbf{I}}_{i^{\prime}\!/}

we conclude that

\|\kappa(\bar{f})\|_{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}}\leq\|\bar{f}\|_{{\mathbf{D}}}\,.

This shows that
\kappa
extends by continuity to a functor

\kappa\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}
which is necessarily inverse to
\sigma
.
∎

As a first application of Proposition
7.15
we show Lemma
7.18
below. Its specialization to
C^{*}
-algebras has been used in the proof of Proposition
3.8
.

Let
{\mathbf{I}}
be a small filtered category.

Definition 7.16
.

We say that
{\mathbf{I}}
is countably filtered if for every functor
\mathbf{J}\to{\mathbf{I}}
from a countable category the inclusion
\mathbf{J}\to{\mathbf{I}}
extends to the cone over
\mathbf{J}
.

Example 7.17
.

If
A
is a
C^{*}
-algebra, then the poset of all separable subalgebras of
A
is countably filtered.
If
\mathbf{J}
is a subset of this poset, then we can extend the inclusion to the cone over
\mathbf{J}
by
sending the cone tip to the separable subalgebra
\overline{\bigcup_{j\in\mathbf{J}}A_{j}}
of
A
, where the closure is taken in
A
.

By the same argument, if
G
is a countable group, then the poset of separable
G
-invariant subalgebras of
A
is countably filtered.
More generally, if
{\mathbf{C}}
is a
G
-
C^{*}
-category, then the poset of
separable
G
-invariant subcategories is countably filtered.

∎

Let
{\mathbf{C}}\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
be a diagram indexed by a small filtered category
{\mathbf{I}}
,
i
be in
{\mathbf{I}}
, and
{\mathbf{D}}
be a subcategory of
{\mathbf{C}}_{i}
.

Lemma 7.18
.

Assume:

1.

{\mathbf{D}}
is separable.

2.

The composition
{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}_{i}\xrightarrow{\iota_{i}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}
is zero.

3.

{\mathbf{I}}
is countably filtered.

Then there exists a morphism
\phi\colon i\to i^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{I}}
such that the composition

{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}_{i}\xrightarrow{\phi(i\to i^{\prime})}{\mathbf{C}}_{i^{\prime}}
is zero.

Proof.

Using the Assumption
1
on
{\mathbf{D}}
we can choose a countable set of morphisms
M
in
{\mathbf{D}}
such that

M\cap{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(D,D^{\prime})
is dense for every pair of objects
D,D^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{D}}
. By the description of the norm in
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}
given by Proposition
7.15
and Assumption
2
,
for every
m
in
M
and
n
in
{\rm Nature}
we can find
\phi_{m,n}\colon i\to i_{m,n}
in
{\mathbf{I}}_{i/}
such that

\|\phi_{m,n}(m)\|_{C_{i^{\prime\prime}}}\leq\frac{1}{n}
. We let
\mathbf{J}
be the subcategory of
{\mathbf{I}}
with the set of objects
\{i_{m,n}\>|\>m\in M,n\in{\rm Nature}\}
and the non-identity morphisms
\phi_{m,n}\colon i\to i^{\prime}
. Using that
{\mathbf{I}}
is countably filtered by Assumption
3
we can now extend the inclusion of
\mathbf{J}
into
{\mathbf{I}}
to the cone over
\mathbf{J}

such that the cone tip is sent to an object
i^{\prime}
of
{\mathbf{I}}
. We let
\phi\colon i\to i^{\prime}
be the unique morphism which factorizes as

i\xrightarrow{\phi_{m,n}}i_{m,n}\to i^{\prime}
for every
m
in
M
and
n
in
{\rm Nature}
such that the second morphism belongs to this extension.

For every
m
in
M
and
n
in
{\rm Nature}
we have by construction
{\mathbf{C}}(\phi)(m)=0
.
By the density assumption on
M
this implies that
C(\phi)
annihilates all morphisms of
{\mathbf{D}}
.
∎

A second application of Proposition
7.15
ist the following. For
C^{*}
-categories, a faithful functor is the analogue of an isometric inclusion of
C^{*}
-algebras.

Corollary 7.19
.

Filtered colimits in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserve faithful functors.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbf{I}}
be a small filtered
category and
({\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{B}})\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}

be a natural transformation of functors such that
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\to{\mathbf{B}}_{i}
is faithful for every
i
in
{\mathbf{I}}
. Then we must show that
the induced morphism
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{A}}\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{B}}
is faithful.
But this immediately follows from the explicit description of the norm on the colimits given in Proposition
7.15
.
∎

Lemma 7.20
.

Filtered colimits in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserve exact sequences.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbf{I}}
be a small filtered
category and consider a diagram of exact sequences

0\to{\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{B}}\to{\mathbf{C}}\to 0

in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}

indexed by
{\mathbf{I}}
.
We must show that the sequence

0\to{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,{\mathbf{A}}\to{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,{\mathbf{B}}\to{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,{\mathbf{C}}\to 0

is exact.

By the definition of an exact sequence in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}

we have bijections
\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{A}})\cong\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{B}})\cong\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{C}})
. For a set
X
we let
0[X]
denote the
C^{*}
-category with the set of objects
X
and only zero morphisms.
We write the diagram of exact sequences as a diagram
of squares

\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0[\mathrm{Ob}({\mathbf{B}})]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}

in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}

which are cartesian and cocartesian.
The colimit of cocartesian squares

\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0[\mathrm{Ob}(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{B}})]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}}

(7.13)

is again cocartesian, where we exploit that the two functors
0[-]\colon{\mathbf{Set}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and
\mathrm{Ob}(-)\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
are both left adjoints by
[
Bun
, Lem. 3.8.1 & 3.8.2]
and therefore commute with colimits in order to calculate the lower left corner in the colimit. This in particular implies that the image of the functor
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{A}}\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{B}}

is the kernel of the functor
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{B}}\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}}
. In order to show that the square in (
7.13
) is also cartesian it therefore suffices to show that
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{A}}\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{B}}
is isometric. This is exactly the assertion of Corollary
7.19
which is applicable here since the inclusions
{\mathbf{A}}_{i}\to{\mathbf{B}}_{i}
are isometric for all
i
in
{\mathbf{I}}
.
∎

The following technical lemma is used in the proof of Lemma
7.13
.

Lemma 7.21
.

The minimal tensor product on
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves isometric inclusions.

Proof.

If
A^{\prime}\to A
is an isometric inclusion, then we must show that

{A^{\prime}}\otimes_{\min}B\to{A}\otimes_{\min}B
is again an isometric inclusion. We choose faithful representations of
\alpha
and
\beta
of
A
and
B
as above, respectively.
Then we can use
\alpha_{|A^{\prime}}
as a faithful representation of
A^{\prime}
.
The assertion is now clear from (
7.6
).
∎

We can now prove Lemma
7.13
and hence complete the proof of Proposition
7.10
.
We discuss the cases
?=\min
and
?=\max
separately.

Proof of Lemma
7.13
in the case
?=\min
.

Let
{\mathbf{I}}
be a filtered category,

A\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
be a diagram, and
B
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
Then we consider the canonical map

{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,(A\otimes_{\min}B)\to({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,A)\otimes_{\min}B\,.

(7.14)

We must show that it is an isomorphism.

Since the structure maps of the diagram
A
are assumed to be isometric inclusions it follows from the explicit
description of the colimit given by Proposition
7.15
that the canonical maps

A_{i}\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A
are isometric inclusions. By the Lemma
7.21
the homomorphisms

A_{i}\otimes_{\min}B\to{(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A)}\otimes_{\min}B
are isometric inclusions, too.
Similarly, the structure maps of the system
A\otimes_{\min}B
are isometric inclusions, and hence

A_{i}\otimes_{\min}B\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}(A\otimes_{\min}B)
is an isometric inclusion.
This implies by the Proposition
7.15
that the canonical map (
7.14
)
is an isometry. Since its
image clearly contains the dense subset
{({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}}^{{}^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}A)}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B

we conclude that it is an isomorphism.
∎

The argument for proof of Lemma
7.13

in the case of
\otimes_{\min}
can be used to deduce
the following general statement. We let
\mathbf{M},{\mathbf{N}}
be in
\{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}},C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\}
, and we consider two groups
G,H
and a functor

F\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathbf{M})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BH,{\mathbf{N}})
.

Proposition 7.22
.

Assume:

1.

F
preserves faithful functors.
11
11

11

In the case of
C^{*}
-algebras we interpret
faithful functor
as
isometric inclusion
.

2.

For any filtered diagram
{\mathbf{C}}\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathbf{M})

the images of
F({\mathbf{C}}(i))\to F(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}})
for all
i
in
{\mathbf{I}}
together generate

F(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}})
.

Then
F

preserves filtered colimits
of diagrams whose structure maps are faithful functors.

Proof.

One argues as in the proof of Lemma
7.13

in the case of
\otimes_{\min}
replacing
-\otimes_{\min}B
by

C
that
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}F({\mathbf{C}})\to F(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{C}})

is faithful. Then Assumption
2
implies that this map is an isomorphism.
∎

Proof of Lem.
7.13
in the case
?=\max
.

In this argument we use that
\otimes_{\max}
preserves exact sequences of
C^{*}
-algebras in each argument, see e.g.
[
BO08
, Prop. 3.7.1]
.
Let
{\mathbf{I}}
be a filtered category,

A\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
be a diagram, and
B
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
Then we consider the canonical map

{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,(A\otimes_{\max}B)\to({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,A)\otimes_{\max}B\,.

(7.15)

We must show that it is an isomorphism.

We first consider the special case that
B
is in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}
and
A\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}
. The latter condition means that
A_{i}
is unital for every
i
, and for every morphism
i\to i^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{I}}
the structure map
A_{i}\to A_{i^{\prime}}
preserves units.
To prove that (
7.15
) is an isomorphism in this case, it suffices to show that every homomorphism

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}(A\otimes_{\max}B)\to T
for every
T
in
{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}
factorizes over a homomorphism

(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A)\otimes_{\max}B\to T
.

Let

\rho\colon\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}(A\otimes_{\max}B)\to T
be a homomorphism.
It determines a compatible family of homomorphisms

(\rho_{i}\colon A_{i}\otimes_{\max}B\to T)_{i\in{\mathbf{I}}}
.
Using the unit of
B
we construct
a compatible family of homomorphisms
(\pi_{i}\colon A_{i}\to T)_{i\in{\mathbf{I}}}
by

\pi_{i}(a)\coloneqq\rho_{i}(a\otimes 1_{B})
.
This family induces a homomorphism
\pi\colon\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A\to T
.
For every
i
in
{\mathbf{I}}
we can construct a homomorphism
\kappa_{i}\colon B\to T

by
\kappa_{i}(b)\coloneqq\rho_{i}(1_{A_{i}}\otimes b)
. Because
{\mathbf{I}}
is connected, these homomorphisms are independent of
i
.
We will just write
\kappa(b)\coloneqq\kappa_{i}(b)
for any choice.
Note that
\rho_{i}(a\otimes b)=\pi_{i}(a)\kappa(b)
for all
i
in
{\mathbf{I}}
and
a
in
A_{i}
,
b
in
B
.
Then we get a map

\rho^{\prime}\colon(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A)\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B\to T
determined by
\rho^{\prime}(a\otimes b)\coloneqq\pi(a)\kappa(b)
.
By the universal property of the maximal tensor product
\rho^{\prime}
extends to
\rho^{\prime\prime}\colon(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A)\otimes_{\max}B\to T
. The homomorphism
\rho
factorizes over
\rho^{\prime\prime}
as desired.

We now consider the case of a diagram
A\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
but still assume that
B
is unital. We have a split unitalization exact sequence
0\to A\to A^{+}\to{\mathbb{C}}\to 0
. Applying to this sequence
-\otimes_{\max}B
we get again a diagram of exact sequences. We consider the diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}(A\otimes_{\max}B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}(A^{+}\otimes_{\max}B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A)\otimes_{\max}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A^{+})\otimes_{\max}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

The upper horizontal sequence is exact by Proposition
7.20
.
The right horizontal maps are obtained from the canonical maps
A^{+}_{i}\to{\mathbb{C}}
by tensoring with
B
.
The middle vertical map is an isomorphism by the unital case of this lemma shown above.
We now argue that the lower horizontal sequence is exact, which will imply that the arrow marked by
!
is an isomorphism.
We have an equivalence of categories
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}_{/{\mathbb{C}}}
given by
A\mapsto(A^{+}\to{\mathbb{C}})
, and whose inverse is given by
(\phi\colon B\to{\mathbb{C}})\mapsto{\mathtt{ker}}(\phi)
. Furthermore, the canonical functor
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}_{/{\mathbb{C}}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}
preserves colimits in view of the adjunction

((A\to{\mathbb{C}})\mapsto A):C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}_{/{\mathbb{C}}}\rightleftarrows C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}:(B\mapsto(B\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}))\,.

Hence

{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,A\cong{\mathtt{ker}}({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,(A^{+}\to{\mathbb{C}}))\cong{\mathtt{ker}}({\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,A^{+}\to{\mathbb{C}})

and we have the split exact sequence

0\to{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,A\to{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}\,A^{+}\to{\mathbb{C}}\to 0\,.

We finally use that
-\otimes_{\max}B
preserves exact sequences.

We finally allow
B
to be non-unital. Then we get a diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}(A\otimes_{\max}B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}(A\otimes_{\max}B^{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A)\otimes_{\max}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A)\otimes_{\max}B^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

The lower horizontal sequence is exact since
(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathbf{I}}}A)\otimes_{\max}-

preserves exact sequences.
The middle vertical morphism is an isomorphism by the case considered above since
B^{+}
is unital.
The upper horizontal sequence is again exact by Proposition
7.20
.
We again conclude that the arrow marked by
!
is an isomorphism.
∎

We now use Proposition
7.10
in order to extend various results from
C^{*}
-algebras to
C^{*}
-categories.

Proposition 7.23
.

1.

The maximal tensor product on
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves exact sequences.

2.

The minimal tensor product on
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves faithful functors.

Proof.

The analogue of Assertion
1
for
C^{*}
-algebras is
well-known, see e.g.
[
BO08
, Prop. 3.7.1]
. Let

0\to{\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{B}}\to{\mathbf{C}}\to 0

(7.16)

be an exact sequence in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, and let
{\mathbf{D}}
be in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. We must show that the
sequence

0\to{\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{D}}\to 0

(7.17)

is exact.
The functor

A\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{i}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves exact sequences
[
Bun
, Prop. 8.9.2]
.
Applying
A
to the exact sequence (
7.16
)
we get the exact sequence

0\to A({\mathbf{A}})\to A({\mathbf{B}})\to A({\mathbf{C}})\to 0

in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
Since the maximal tensor product in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves exact sequences the sequence

0\to A({\mathbf{A}})\otimes_{\max}A({\mathbf{D}})\to A({\mathbf{B}})\otimes_{\max}A({\mathbf{D}})\to A({\mathbf{C}})\otimes_{\max}A({\mathbf{D}})\to 0

is exact. By
Proposition
7.10
in the case of
?=\max
(whose proof has been completed already)
the sequence

0\to A({\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{D}})\to A({\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{D}})\to A({\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{D}})\to 0

is then also exact. We now employ that the functor
A
detects exactness. In order to see this, note that for all pairs of objects
B,B^{\prime}
of
{\mathbf{B}}
and
D,D^{\prime}
of
{\mathbf{D}}
we can conclude that the sequence of complex vector spaces

\displaystyle\mathclap{0\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{A}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{D}}}((B,D),(B^{\prime},D^{\prime}))\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{B}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{D}}}((B,D),(B^{\prime},D^{\prime}))\to{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{\max}{\mathbf{D}}}((B,D),(B^{\prime},D^{\prime}))\to 0}

is exact, since these morphism spaces are direct summands of the corresponding algebras by
[
Joa03
, Lem. 3.6]
or
[
Bun
, Lem. 6.7]
. Here we use that the maps in an exact seqence of
C^{*}
-categories are bijective on objects so that we can interpret, e.g.,
B
also as an object of
{\mathbf{A}}
or
{\mathbf{C}}
. Consequently, the sequence in (
7.17
) is exact, too.

For Assertion
2
, we consider a faithful functor
\phi\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
and
{\mathbf{E}}
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. We first assume that
\phi
is injective on objects.
Then we can form the commutative square

Since the functor
A
preserves isometric inclusions by
[
Bun
, Lem. 6.8]
, it follows from Lemma

that the lower horizontal map is isometric. We conclude that the upper horizontal arrow is isometric, too.
We now use that
A
detects isometric inclusions.
In detail, for any objects
C,C^{\prime}
of
{\mathbf{C}}
and
E,E^{\prime}
of
{\mathbf{E}}

we have a commutative square of Banach spaces

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-29.33334pt\raise-32.00891pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
